Résumédes travaux de thèse [SOURCE: Page_10]

Ces dernières années, les événements climatiques extrêmes, en particulier les sécheresses printanières et estivales prolongées, représentent des risques importants pour les forêts, conduisant àdes dépérissements qui touchent de nombreuses espèces. En particulier, ces changements climatiques entraînent une diminution des populations de hêtre (Fagus sylvatica L.) àtravers l'Europe. Par conséquent, le développement de stratégies visant à renforcer la résilience des hêtres face à la sécheresse est devenu crucial. Notre recherche a exploré si Cenococcum geophilum, un champignon ascomycète ectomycorhizien cosmopolite, pouvait tolérer des conditions de sécheresse et aider ses plantes hôtes, en particulier le hêtre, às'adapter au manque d'eau. Par criblage phénotypique de souches fongiques, nous avons identifiéune souche de C. geophilum, potentiellement tolérante à la sécheresse, en testant la croissance des isolats sur un milieu de croissance contenant des concentrations accrues de polyéthylène glycol (PEG) comme substitut du stress hydrique du sol ; le PEG augmentant la pression osmotique des cellules. Nous avons ensuite analyséle transcriptome de cette souche cultivé sur PEG à différents intervalles et évalué la récupération de son expression génique après un retour aux conditions normales. Six heures après le traitement au PEG, l'expression génique a changéde manière spectaculaire, activant des voies connues d'adaptation au stress osmotique et de détoxification. Le transcriptome de l'isolat est revenu àson état initial deux jours après son transfert sur un milieu de culture dépourvu de PEG. Étant donnéla tolérance de l'isolat àdes changements majeurs de pression osmotique et sa récupération rapide, nous avons menéune expérience en serre pour déterminer si une inoculation mycorhizienne contrôlée avec cette souche pouvait améliorer la résistance des plantules de hêtre à la suspension de l'irrigation. Bien que la mycorhization contrôlée ait augmentéle taux de survie des semis, les isolats naturels de C. geophilum présents dans le sol des cultures ont surpassénotre souche sélectionnée, probablement en raison de la brève durée de l'expérience et de la formation limitée de nouvelles racines latérales. Pour approfondir les mécanismes moléculaires de la tolérance à la sécheresse de [SOURCE: Page_10]

1 [SOURCE: Page_10]

l’association symbiotique Cenococcum-Fagus, nous avons tenté de développer un système in vitro de culture reposant sur la micro-propagation de plantules de hêtre. Cependant, toutes les lignées de hêtre testées ont montré une formation réduite de racines latérales et de faibles taux de colonisation avec cinq champignons ectomycorhiziens, y compris C. geophilum. Bien qu’aucune ectomycorhize typique n’a pu être observé, nous avons mise en évidence par microscopie confocale la formation de structures intraradiculaires semblables à celles développées par les champignons endophytes. Pour de futures études, nous recommandons de sélectionner des lignées de hêtre avec de meilleures capacités d'enracinement (Chapitre 4) et d'élargir le nombre d'isolats de C. geophilum testés (Chapitres 3 et 4), en isolant des souches adaptées localement. [SOURCE: Page_11]

Objectifs de la thèse [SOURCE: Page_11]

Les recherches entreprises dans ce projet visaient àétudier les mécanismes moléculaires développés par le champignon ectomycorhizien Cenococcum geophilum afin de tolérer la sécheresse édaphique du sol. Ces recherches ont été conduites sur le mycélium végétatif libre, ainsi que sur l’association C. geophilum-Fagus sylvatica. Nos objectifs principaux étaient : 1) Identifier une souche de C. geophilum ayant une forte tolérance à la sécheresse, c'est-à-dire capable de croî tre àde fortes concentrations de PEG. 2) Analyser les modifications du transcriptome suite au stress hydrique (principalement le stress osmotique, simulépar un traitement au PEG) et la récupération subséquente sous des conditions normales. 3) Utiliser la souche résistante à la sécheresse dans une étude en serre impliquant une mycorhization contrôlée de jeunes hêtres. 4) Développer un système in vitro pour établir des ectomycorhizes entre C. geophilum et F. sylvatica afin de faciliter de futures investigations moléculaires. [SOURCE: Page_11]

2 [SOURCE: Page_11]

Présentation simplifiée du manuscript de thèse [SOURCE: Page_12]

Dans le chapitre 1, je présente une synthèse bibliographique de nos connaissances actuelles sur le champignon symbiotique C. geophilum, en me concentrant sur ses aspects biologiques, génétiques et écologiques. Les principaux sujets abordés incluent : [SOURCE: Page_12]

Aire écologique et distribution mondiale : C. geophilum est un champignon ectomycorhizien ubiquitaire qui forme des relations symbiotiques avec plus de 200 espèces de plantes, englobant des arbres, des arbustes et des plantes herbacées. Il prospère dans des écosystèmes variés, incluant les forêts boréales, tempérées, subtropicales et les savanes. Bien qu'il soit fréquent dans les environnements alpins et arctiques, il est moins répandu dans les régions tropicales, à l’exception de certains lieux comme la Thaïlande. Réputé pour son adaptabilité, C. geophilum peut survivre à différentes profondeurs du sol et est particulièrement abondant dans les écosystèmes pionniers, tels que les moraines glaciaires nouvellement exposées et les déserts volcaniques. Il joue un rôle crucial dans la succession forestière précoce, s’associant aux semis et aux plantes matures, et est fréquent dans les écosystèmes alpins grâce à sa production de sclérotes. Le champignon conserve sa dominance dans les jeunes peuplements forestiers comme dans les peuplements établis, même après des incendies. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Structure et développement : C. geophilum, un champignon mélanisé caractérisé par des hyphes septés dématiacés riches en mélanine, démontre une résistance exceptionnelle aux contraintes environnementales. Cette résilience lui permet de prospérer dans des conditions difficiles où d’autres champignons mycorhiziens échouent. Le champignon produit des sclérotes mélanisées pouvant survivre jusqu’à 40 ans, tandis que ses racines ectomycorhiziennes peuvent persister dans le sol pendant environ trois ans. C. geophilum synthétise la mélanine via les voies DOPA et DHN, la voie DOPA étant plus active dans les racines ectomycorhizées. Morphologiquement semblable à d’autres endophytes racinaires noirs septés, l’identification précise de C. geophilum nécessite des techniques morphologiques couplées àdes techniques de génotypage moléculaire. [SOURCE: Page_12]

3 [SOURCE: Page_12]

Le champignon forme également des sclérotes qui agissent comme réserves de nutriments et aident à survivre dans des conditions extrêmes, contribuant au stock de glucides dans les sols forestiers. Bien que des études moléculaires ont mis en évidence des gènes de compatibilité sexuelle (mating type) dans le génome de ce champignon ascomycète, aucune structure sexuelle n’a été observée jusqu’à présent. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Variation génétique et complexe d’espèces : Malgré sa reproduction asexuée supposée, le champignon présente une diversité génétique significative. Les analyses phylogénétiques ont révéléque C. geophilum est très probablement un complexe d’espèces comprenant plusieurs lignées cryptiques. Cette variabilité génétique contribue probablement à son adaptabilité et à son succès dans des environnements diversifiés. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Tolérance au stress : C. geophilum fait preuve d’une adaptabilité impressionnante à diverses contraintes environnementales, notamment la sécheresse, la chaleur, le froid et les métaux lourds, soulignant son importance écologique. Ce symbiote est reconnu pour sa tolérance àla sécheresse, étant fréquemment retrouvé dans des environnements soumis à des sécheresses édaphiques. Sa tolérance à la sécheresse du sol est appuyée par diverses études examinant les réponses physiologiques et l’expression des gènes liés au stress, bien que les mécanismes spécifiques demeurent en grande partie inconnus. Des facteurs tels que l’accumulation d’osmolytes compatibles (polyols), les protéines de stress thermique et les canaux membranaires de la famille des aquaporines sont supposés contribuer àsa résilience. Cependant, des recherches récentes remettent en question son efficacitéen tant que symbiote résistant àla sécheresse, révélant une abondance réduite chez les plantules mycorhiziennes et dans divers écosystèmes forestiers durant les périodes de sécheresse. Ces études indiquent également que les gènes associés à la tolérance à la sécheresse ne seraient pas les plus abondants dans les sols plus secs, suggérant une interaction plus complexe – multifactorielle – entre C. geophilum et le stress hydrique. Les études en laboratoire montrent que si des températures de 26°C peuvent inhiber la croissance de certains isolats, l’espèce peut néanmoins former des associations mycorhiziennes après une brève exposition àun stress thermique autour de [SOURCE: Page_13]

4 [SOURCE: Page_13]

70°C. Cependant, une combinaison de stress hydrique et de chaleur àdes températures élevées peut être néfaste. L’abondance relative de C. geophilum et ses activités exoenzymatiques sont également influencées par la température et la sécheresse, en particulier en association avec différentes espèces de chêne. En revanche, C. geophilum montre une résistance remarquable face au froid, restant viable à des températures de congélation de -80°C et prospérant àdes températures inférieures à1°C, probablement grâce àsa capacitéàsynthétiser du mannitol, un polyol qui offre une protection contre les conditions difficiles. De plus, l’espèce présente une adaptation locale aux concentrations de métaux lourds dans les sols ultramafiques. [SOURCE: Page_14]

Relations symbiotiques et préférences pour les hôtes : C. geophilum forme des associations ectomycorhiziennes avec un large éventail d’espèces végétales, y compris des arbres, des arbustes et des plantes herbacées. Il peut établir des ecto- et des ectendomycorrhizes, montrant une flexibilitédans sa symbiose avec divers hôtes. Nous avons résuméici ses hôtes potentiels et les caractéristiques de leurs mycorhizes avec C. geophilum et fourni des éléments de preuve ainsi que des hypothèses sur ses préférences pour certains hôtes. [SOURCE: Page_14]

Génomique et biologie moléculaire : Le génome de C. geophilum, d'environ 178 Mb, est l’un des plus grands parmi les champignons ectomycorhizien, avec une quantité significative de séquences répétées, comme les transposons, et des protéines effectrices secrétées qui sont certainement impliquées dans les relations symbiotiques avec ses plantes hôtes. Des études sur l’expression génique indiquent que 3 % de ses gènes sont induits lors de la symbiose, en particulier ceux liés aux transporteurs et aux petites protéines sécrétées (SSP), qui peuvent agir comme effecteurs pour influencer les mécanismes de défense des plantes. Les recherches en génomique des populations révèlent une structure génétique complexe avec des lignées distinctes présentant à la fois des traits clonaux et recombinants, façonnés par des facteurs géographiques et environnementaux. Les populations de C. geophilum montrent des adaptations locales àdes niches écologiques spécifiques, les populations associées aux angiospermes affichant une plus grande diversité génétique que celles associées aux gymnospermes. Ces découvertes [SOURCE: Page_14]

5 [SOURCE: Page_14]

génomiques soulignent l’importance écologique de ce champignon dans le cycle des nutriments, l'amélioration de la résistance àla sécheresse et le maintien de la stabilité des écosystèmes. [SOURCE: Page_15]

Applications en foresterie et en remédiation environnementale : C. geophilum pourrait être utiliséen gestion forestière dans le cadre des applications en foresterie durable, notamment pour améliorer la résistance à la sécheresse et l’absorption des nutriments chez les arbres. La mycorhization impliquant ce champignon pourrait également être utilisépour la restauration des terres dégradées et dans les efforts de bioremédiation, comme le maintien des sols contaminés par les métaux lourds. Sa résilience face aux contraintes environnementales en fait un outil précieux pour s’adapter aux changements climatiques. [SOURCE: Page_15]

Pistes de recherche futures : Nous soulignons également plusieurs pistes de recherche future, notamment une meilleure compréhension des mécanismes génétiques derrière sa tolérance au stress, sa spécificité envers les hôtes et la génomique des populations. Il est également suggéré d’explorer davantage le rôle de ce champignon ectomycorhizien dans le cycle des nutriments, ses interactions avec les communautés microbiennes du sol et son potentiel pour améliorer la résilience des forêts face aux changements environnementaux mondiaux. [SOURCE: Page_15]

Dans le chapitre 2, intitulé «Modifications de l'expression génique dans une souche de Cenococcum geophilum tolérante à la sécheresse durant le stress osmotique et les périodes de récupération », je résume nos travaux sur l’impact du stress osmotique sur l’expression génique du champignon. En effet, dans le contexte du changement climatique, le stress hydrique pose des défis considérables aux écosystèmes forestiers. Le champignon ectomycorhizien C. geophilum a étéfréquemment observédans les sols forestiers soumis au stress hydrique, montrant une résilience accrue àla sécheresse (stress osmotique) par rapport àd'autres champignons ectomycorhiziens, et il peut favoriser la croissance de son hôte dans des [SOURCE: Page_15]

6 [SOURCE: Page_15]

conditions défavorables. Cependant, les mécanismes moléculaires sous-jacents à l'adaptation de ce symbiote au stress hydrique restent àélucider. Nous avons examiné les effets des changements de pression osmotique (utilisée comme substitut au stress hydrique) sur l'isolat C. geophilum 1.093, identifié comme tolérant àla sécheresse. Une analyse par séquençage d'ARN a étéutilisée pour identifier les gènes liés au stress osmotique à travers l'analyse transcriptomique du mycélium cultivésur du polyéthylène glycol (PEG), utilisépour augmenter la pression osmotique. Le mycélium végétatif cultivéen milieu liquide, avec ou sans PEG, a étéprélevéà6, 24 et 48 h. Ensuite, le mycélium restant a ététransférédans un milieu sans PEG pendant 24 et 48 h pour évaluer sa capacitéde récupération. Le traitement au PEG a considérablement modifiél'expression des gènes dès les six premières heures d'exposition. À chaque moment, un groupe distinct de gènes a montré une régulation positive ou négative importante. Néanmoins, un ensemble commun de transcrits différemment exprimés a été identifié à tous les moments, comprenant des gènes codant pour des transporteurs membranaires et des enzymes impliquées dans le métabolisme lipidique. Ces gènes sont rapidement revenus àdes niveaux d'expression constitutifs après le transfert vers un milieu sans PEG. L'isolat C. geophilum 1.093, connu pour sa tolérance au stress osmotique, a montré des altérations significatives de l'expression génique en réponse aux changements de pression osmotique, un substitut au stress hydrique. De plus, ce champignon a démontré la capacité à retrouver rapidement sa viabilité après être revenu à des conditions favorables. [SOURCE: Page_16]

Dans le chapitre 3, intitulé«Effet de l'inoculation contrôlée de C. geophilum 1.093 sur la communauté ectomycorhizienne naturelle du hêtre (Fagus sylvatica L.) sous conditions de sécheresse. », nous avons cherché à déterminer si la mycorhization contrôlée avec C. geophilum 1.093 pouvait renforcer la protection des plantules de hêtre, déjà mycorhizées par un microbiome naturel du sol, contre le stress hydrique. Une expérience pilote d'inoculation a été menée en serre en utilisant l'isolat tolérant à la sécheresse C. geophilum 1.093 et des semis de hêtre européen. Le stress hydrique a été induit en réduisant l'humidité du sol dans des pots contenant des plantules inoculées. Après la sécheresse expérimentale, les morphotypes des racines ectomycorhizées ont été [SOURCE: Page_16]

7 [SOURCE: Page_16]

caractérisés, et pour certains morphotypes sélectionnés, leurs taux de colonisation ont été quantifiés. Les résultats ont révélé que l'isolat 1.093 de C. geophilum a pu former des associations ectomycorhiziennes avec les plantules de hêtre. Cependant, dans cette configuration expérimentale, aucun effet positif de l'inoculation contrôlée avec C. geophilum^1.093 sur le taux de survie des plantules de hêtre n'a étéobservé. Ainsi, nous recommandons de tester d'autres isolats provenant de différents clades ou des souches localement adaptées de C. geophilum dans les recherches futures. [SOURCE: Page_17]

Dans le chapitre 4, intitulé: “Établissement d'associations ectomycorhiziennes entre des champignons ectomycorhiziens, y compris Cenococcum geophilum, et des semis de hêtre (Fagus sylvatica L.) àl'aide d'un système expérimental in vitro. », nous avons testé la mycorhization du hêtre. En effet, au cours des dernières décennies, plusieurs systèmes expérimentaux in vitro ont été développés pour explorer les mécanismes moléculaires impliqués dans le développement et le fonctionnement de la symbiose ectomycorhizienne. Cependant, aucun système in vitro n’a été mis en place pour étudier l'ectomycorhize du hêtre, malgrél'existence de protocoles de micro-propagation in vitro des boutures de hêtre. Nous avons donc testé différentes méthodes pour obtenir des cultures in vitro de hêtre à partir de divers explants, y compris des graines et des branches provenant de forêts locales. Des tests préliminaires ont également étéréalisés afin d'établir la symbiose entre des boutures in vitro de lignées de hêtre et divers champignons ectomycorhiziens. Nos efforts ont permis le développement réussi d'un système d'enracinement in vitro pour plusieurs lignées de F. sylvatica (FS-ap, FS-10-4) et de Fagus orientalis (FO-BS, FO Clone^3, FO Clone^30) obtenues grâce aux Dr Castro et Martínez du CSIC (Espagne). La lignée de hêtre FS-ap peut former un manteau fongique, mais ne développe pas de réseau de Hartig en présence des champignons ectomycorhiziens sélectionnés, tels que les souches de C. geophilum 1.093 et 1.058, Laccaria bicolor S238N, ou Lactarius subdulcis 3. Nous avons toutefois observé par microscopie confocale la formation de structures typiques des champignons endophytes dans les extrémités racinaires de la lignée FS-ap colonisées par C. geophilum 1.093. [SOURCE: Page_17]

Enfin, dans le Chapitre 5, je discute l’ensemble de mes résultats et mes conclusions principales. Je propose également quelques perspectives pour de futures recherches, comme le développement [SOURCE: Page_17]

8 [SOURCE: Page_17]

This genetic variability may contribute to its adaptability and extensive distribution across habitats from circumpolar to tropical biomes. These lineages exhibit potential host preferences, suggesting a degree of specialization within the complex. The nuclear genome of C. geophilum has been sequenced, providing valuable insights into the symbiont genetic traits. Notably, this genome encodes a large set of repeated sequences and effector-like small secreted proteins. Transcriptomics has been used to identify candidate genes related to symbiosis and adaptation to environmental stress. ## [EMAIL_REDACTED]

Abstract Background Drought stress presents a significant challenge to forest ecosystems in the context of [SOURCE: Page_35]

climate change. The ectomycorrhizal fungus Cenococcum geophilum was frequently observed in forest [SOURCE: Page_35]

soils experiencing drought stress, demonstrating enhanced resilience to drought (osmotic stress) relative [SOURCE: Page_35]

to other ectomycorrhizal fungi, which could facilitate host growth under adverse conditions. Nevertheless, [SOURCE: Page_35]

the molecular mechanisms underlying the adaptation of C. geophilum to drought stress remain unclear. [SOURCE: Page_35]

In the present study, we examined the effects of changes in osmotic pressure (utilized as a proxy for [SOURCE: Page_35]

drought stress) on the isolate C. geophilum 1.093, which was previously identified as drought-tolerant. [SOURCE: Page_35]

RNA sequencing was used to identify osmotic stress-related genes through transcriptome analysis of [SOURCE: Page_35]

mycelia cultivated on polyethylene glycol (PEG), which was used to increase osmotic pressure. [SOURCE: Page_35]

Vegetative mycelia grown in liquid growth medium with or without PEG, were sampled at 6, 24, and 48 h. [SOURCE: Page_35]

Subsequently, the remaining mycelia were transferred to growth medium without PEG for 24 and 48 h to [SOURCE: Page_35]

assess their recovery ability. [SOURCE: Page_35]

Results PEG treatment significantly altered gene expression as early as six hours after exposure. At each [SOURCE: Page_35]

time point, a distinct group of genes exhibited substantial regulation. A common set of differentially [SOURCE: Page_35]

expressed transcripts was identified across all time points, encompassing genes encoding membrane [SOURCE: Page_35]

transporters and enzymes involved in lipid metabolism. These genes rapidly reverted to constitutive [SOURCE: Page_35]

expression levels upon transfer to the PEG-free medium. [SOURCE: Page_35]

26 [SOURCE: Page_35]

Conclusions C. geophilum isolate 1.093, selected for its tolerance to osmotic stress, exhibited significant [SOURCE: Page_36]

alterations in gene expression in response to changes in osmotic pressure, a proxy for drought stress. [SOURCE: Page_36]

Furthermore, this fungus demonstrated its capacity to rapidly regained viability upon transitioning back to [SOURCE: Page_36]

favorable conditions. [SOURCE: Page_36]

Keywords Cenococcum geophilum; drought; ectomycorrhiza; transcriptome [SOURCE: Page_36]

Background Drylands account for approximately 40% of total land area [1]. Recently, global warming has led to an increasing probability of extreme climatic events, such as drought, which threaten the stability of forest ecosystems [2,3]. It also contributes to the expansion of global drought-sensitive areas, resulting in forest decline [4]. Furthermore, drought alters the root morphological traits and increases root mortality. A global study of root traits associated with drought demonstrated that drought significantly decreased root length and root length density [5]. These alterations may result in a greater difficulty in nutrient absorption and transport into plants under higher drought intensities, leading to increased mortality. In response to drought, trees modify their underground C allocation and potentially increase their root exudation types and rates [6,7]. This likely affects the shoot growth and tree health. Drought is an increasingly critical issue in forest ecosystems. Ectomycorrhizal fungi establish symbiotic relationships with forest plants and facilitate water acquisition through mycelial networks by expanding the soil contact area [8]. The ascomycetous fungus Cenococcum geophilum forms ectomycorrhizas with various woody plants and is frequently observed in extreme environmental conditions [9,10]. Herzog et al. [11] investigated the symbiotic relationships between C. geophilum and three oak species in response to drought and/or heat treatment. This study demonstrated that both organisms exhibited drought and heat tolerance. Coleman et al. [12] examined the drought resilience of ectomycorrhizal fungi, revealing that certain C. geophilum isolates can grow under -3 MPa and exhibit greater drought resilience compared to other ectomycorrhizal fungi. Although C. geophilum is classified as a short-distance exploring-type ectomycorrhizal fungus, long- or middle-distance exploring-type ectomycorrhizal fungi are considered to support their host in obtaining more water, the reduction of carbon from the host under drought conditions also limits [SOURCE: Page_36]

27 [SOURCE: Page_36]

their growth [13]. The melanized mantle of ectomycorrhizal roots established by C. geophilum may also protect root tips of host plants from desiccation [14, 15]. Furthermore, C. geophilum accumulates high amounts of mannitol as a carbon source [16], a polyol that protects fungi and plants from drought stress [17, 18]. Recent research has demonstrated that several C. geophilum isolates not only exhibit robust growth but also enhance host growth under soil drought conditions [19,20]. These studies revealed that different C. geophilum isolates employ diverse drought resistance strategies. For instance, sensitive isolates absorb Na+ or K+ ions and upregulate peroxisome pathway genes to maintain water potential. Conversely, tolerant isolates upregulated genes encoding the terpenoid-quinone biosynthesis and sphingolipid metabolism pathways. Furthermore, studies have indicated that inoculation with C. geophilum isolates at an early stage can enhance the drought resistance of Pinus massoniana seedlings [20]. However, under laboratory conditions, the drought resilience of symbiotic associations is not tightly correlated with the drought tolerance of fungal isolates [20]. Nevertheless, in a greenhouse experiment utilizing C. geophilum isolate 1.58, it was demonstrated that inoculation with this fungus could facilitate host water acquisition by upregulating genes encoding aquaporins in pine seedlings [21]. Although the resilience of symbiotic plant-fungus associations and drought tolerance of fungal isolates do not always appear to be related, C. geophilum isolates likely adapted to osmotic stress may possess a higher probability than other C. geophilum isolates or ectomycorrhizal fungi that survive in extreme drought environments. Consequently, it is worth assessing whether the presence of C. geophilum contributes to the survival of tree seedlings the experience soil drought stress. Although previous studies have explored C. geophilum drought tolerance mechanisms at the transcriptome level [20], the ability to rapidly recover from drought stress is another crucial aspect of stress management. PEG is commonly employed to simulate drought stress through changes in osmotic pressure of the growth medium. Although drought stress involves various factors, such as changes in soil physical properties that PEG cannot replicate, osmotic stress remains the primary focus when testing drought tolerance in ECM fungi. For instance, Coleman et al. [12] used PEG3350 to examine the drought tolerance of ECM fungi. Similarly, Zhang et al. [22] employed PEG6000 to assess drought tolerance in three ECM fungi and showed that [SOURCE: Page_37]

28 [SOURCE: Page_37]

mild drought stress (-0.15MPa) enhanced their growth. Therefore, in this study, we used PEG to mimic osmotic stress in drought soils. We identified a C. geophilum strain characterized by rapid growth and tolerance to high osmotic pressure (i.e., high PEG levels). Our subsequent objectives were to investigate: (1) how quickly the isolated gene expression changes when exposed to PEG-induced drought stress; (2) which specific pathways and functions are regulated in samples treated with PEG compared to untreated controls; and (3) the nature and speed of regulation of differentially expressed genes following a shift from PEG-containing to PEG-free conditions. [SOURCE: Page_38]

Methods Experiment 1: Fungal strains, Culturing condition and Mycelium growth of Representative Isolates under Drought Treatments Four isolates (Table 1) with different reaction patterns to drought stress were selected from previous studies (not published). C. geophilum strain 1.58 has its whole genome sequenced (https://mycocosm.jgi.doe.gov/Cenge^3/Cenge^3.home.html). The strain 1.58 and 1.278 were provided by Dr. Martina Peter (WSL, Zürich), while isolates BAARN and 1.093 were obtained from the INRAE IAM fungal collection (Champenoux, France). The internal transcribed spacer (ITS) regions of all isolates were sequenced and identified as Cenococcum geophilum by using the pairwise primers ITS1f and ITS$_4$ [23]. [SOURCE: Page_38]

Table 1. Information on the four isolates used in this study Water Strains isolated from Name        Sampling site         Host               potential sclerotia/root tips levels Bärschwil tief, 1.278                             Fagus sylvatica    Wet         Sclerotia Switzerland 1.58        Valais, Switzerland   Pinus sylvestris   Drought     Sclerotia BAARN       Netherlands           Picea abies        Unknown     Unknown 1.093 Tendon, France        Fagus sylvatica    Drought     Sclerotia (Td$_4$-4) [SOURCE: Page_38]

29 [SOURCE: Page_38]

All isolates were maintained in Cg medium [24] with 20 g/L glucose at 20 ◦C in the dark. The solid medium contained 12 g/L agarose. The four isolates were pre- cultured on solid medium on a cellophane piece (diameter = 2 cm) for one month. Sterilized pipet tips (r = 1 cm) were used to cut out sample plugs for use as inocula for preculturing. After preculturing, plugs were removed from the cellophane. Because the high concentration of PEG hindered the solidification of the medium, we transferred the mycelium with cellophane to a liquid medium bead system [25] (Figure 1) to test their growth under drought stress. [SOURCE: Page_39]

Fig. 1. Flowchart for mycelium growth test showing the pre-culturing of the isolates and the bead system. Times points T$_0$, T$_1$, T$_2$, T$_3$, T$_4$: week^0, week^1, week^2, week^3, week^4. The isolates names: 1.58, Baarn, Td$_4$-4, 1.278 means the C. geophilum isolates: 1.058, BAARN, 1.093, 1.278. [SOURCE: Page_39]

The flow chart shows the pre-culturing and testing systems used in this study. The plugs of fungi were precultured on cellophane pieces in a solid medium for half a month to one month. The plug was then removed and the remaining mycelium with a cellophane piece was transferred to the bead system. The bead system consisted of beads, liquid medium with PEG, a slide of square cellophane, and pieces of round cellophane containing C. geophilum strains. [SOURCE: Page_39]

30 [SOURCE: Page_39]

Standard curve correlating PEG-8000 concentrations and water potentials of the medium PEG 8000 (Sigma,           France), a proxy for soil drought conditions, was used to change the osmotic pressure of the medium. To determine the relationship between PEG8000 concentration and medium water potential, liquid Cg medium with increasing PEG8000 concentrations (Figure 2) was measured using a Wescor vapor pressure osmometer 5500 W (WARRANTY, California, US) and Wescor (PSYPRO Water Potential System, USA) (Figure 2) [SOURCE: Page_40]

The equation linking the water potential to the medium PEG concentration is y = 8E-05x^2 - 0.0204x + 2.2548 where y is the water potential (MPa) multiply by-1, x is the weight of PEG8000 in 1 L of Cg liquid medium. [SOURCE: Page_40]

Experimental Design and Mycelium growth After the preculturing on no-PEG solid medium, the small round cellophane with mycelium were transferred to 4 different water potential levels: -0.6MPa (0 g/L), -2MPa (242 g/L), -5MPa (362 g/L), -8MPa (410 g/L). Three replicates were tested for each isolate for each condition. The growth of each isolate was recorded using a NIKON D5200, and pictures were taken from the same position once per week for four weeks. [SOURCE: Page_40]

31 [SOURCE: Page_40]

The images were analyzed using the R package EBImage [26] as previously described [27]. Relative area was measured by counting the pixels of each colony. Data were analyzed using the R package ggplot^2 [28]. Each point represents the actual number of pixels in the image. [SOURCE: Page_41]

Experiment 2: Culture conditions, experimental design, RNA extraction, RNA- Sequencing and data analysis [SOURCE: Page_41]

Culture conditions and experimental design The drought-tolerant isolate 1.093 was selected for this experiment. The mycelium was cultivated in a 1 L Reagent Simax bottle containing 300 ml liquid Cg medium [24]. The bottle was then placed on a shaker for one month at 20 °C in the dark. Following preculture, the mycelium (300 mg/tube) was filtered using a filter (250 mL Sartorius Stedim polycarbonate filter), plastic nets, and pump (N820.3 FTP, KNF LABOPORT, Freiburg im Breisgau, Germany) and subsequently transferred to fresh Cg medium in 50 ml Falcon tubes (30 ml/tubes) for 2 days on a shaker. Fresh mycelia were used as the starting materials. We decided to incubate the mycelium in 210 g/L PEG8000 in Cg medium (corresponding to -1.5MPa), a value close to the -1.2 MPa reported for low soil water potential in beech forests [29]. Each tube contained 300 mg mycelia and 30 ml liquid medium (Quintix Pro Precision Balance QTX120 21RO-1CFR, Göttingen, Germany). The PEG concentration was calculated from the initial experiment (Fig.2). The samples were subsequently filtered and washed thrice with liquid non-PEG medium after 6, 24, and 48 h. Subsequently, 12 tubes of mycelia were transferred back to the no PEG medium and sampled after 24 h and 48 h as recovery samples. Each treatment was performed in independent triplicate (Falcon tubes). (Figure 3). Following sampling, each replicate was divided into three aliquots, frozen in liquid nitrogen, and stored at − 80°C prior to RNA extraction. [SOURCE: Page_41]

32 [SOURCE: Page_41]

Fig. 3. Experimental design to study the effect of PEG treatment on the transcriptome of C. geophilum. Workfow of PEG treatment of C. geophilum mycelium and subsequent transfer to no-PEG medium in order to study the capacity to recover from the treatment. 1.0. 1.093 C.geophilum isolate used in the experiment. R recovery; 0, 0h; 24, 24 h and 48, 48 h. [SOURCE: Page_42]

RNA extraction and RNA-Sequencing Following sample collection, an aliquot from each of the 30 samples was homogenized into powder using steel beads (cooled on liquid N blocks) and a shaker (pre-cooled in -80 ℃ freezer, Retsch GmbH MM400, Haan, Germany). Total RNA was extracted using RNeasy Plant Mini Kit (100) (QIAGEN, Hilden, Germany) and RLT lysis buffer. After DNase treatment (TURBO DNA-free Kit; Thermo Fisher Scientific, France), RNA quality and quantity were assessed using a TapeStation system (Agilent, Santa Clara, California, US). RNA was sequenced using a DNBSEQ-T$_7$ sequencer and the DNBSEQ Eukaryotic Strand-specific Transcriptome Sequencing kit (paired-end 150bp, 20 million clean reads per sample) by BGI Tech Solutions (Poland), according to the manufacturer’s protocols. [SOURCE: Page_42]

RNA-Sequencing analysis Raw reads were trimmed for low quality (quality score, 0.05) and aligned to the C. geophilum reference genome available in the JGI MycoCosm database (Cenge^3, [SOURCE: Page_42]

33 [SOURCE: Page_42]

https://mycocosm.jgi.doe.gov/Cenge^3/Cenge^3.home.html)      using    CLC       Genomics Workbench v^23.05 (Qiagen). For read mapping, the CLC genomic workbench parameters were as follows: minimum length fraction, 0.9; minimum similarity fraction, 0.8; mismatch cost = 2; insertion cost = 3; deletion cost = 3 and the maximum number of hits for a read was set to 10. The unique and total mapped read numbers for each transcript were determined and normalized to RPKM and TPM. Unique mapped reads were utilized as entry files for the identification of differentially expressed genes (DEGs) using DESeq^2 [30]. DEGs were defined as |log^2Foldchange| ≥ 1 and p < 0.05. The complete RNA-Seq data were submitted to NCBI GEO as GEO series xxxxxxx [SOURCE: Page_43]

Bioinformatic analyses An upset plot graph was generated using the R package upsetplot [31] and ggplot^2. Principal Component Analysis (PCA), heatmaps, volcano plot, Gene Ontology Consortium (GO) enrichment graph, Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes (KEGG) enrichment graph, and EuKaryotic Orthologous Groups (KOG) enrichment graphs were generated using the ggfprtify package [32], Complexheatmap package [33], scRNAtoolVis package (Version 0.1.0), TopGo package [34], and Clusterprofiler package [35]. Gene function and annotation files of the reference genome C. geophilum 1.58 (GO, KOG, KEGG, and transporters) were downloaded from the JGI website or directly received from JGI. The polyol metabolism pathway graph was assembled from JGI Mycocosm metabolism pathway graphs. Missing proteins were identified by BlastP search against the Mycocosm Cenococcum genome using Aspergillus fumigatus protein. Weighted co-expression network analysis (WGCNA) was performed using R package WGCNA [36]. After filtering for significantly regulated genes (p-value < 0.05, Fold change > log^21) and removing of low expressed genes (mean unique reads of all conditions< 5 reads), gene expression values were imported into WGCNA to construct co-expression   modules    using   the   automatic   network   construction    function blockwiseModules with default settings, except that the power was set to 14 (Supplementary Figure 1), TOMType was unsigned, mergeCutHeight was 0.25, and minModuleSize was 100. Pearson’s correlation analysis (selecting most related timepoints and gene modules by highest Pearson’s correlation coefficients comparing with others) was used to identify modules significantly associated with different time [SOURCE: Page_43]

34 [SOURCE: Page_43]

points and drought stress treatments. Finally, the module network was mapped and visualized using Cytoscape 3.10.2. [SOURCE: Page_44]

Results PEG treatment affects mycelium growth of the four strains differentially [SOURCE: Page_44]

Fig. 4. Growth of C. geophilum isolates under different water potentials. T$_0$, T$_1$, T$_2$, T$_3$, T$_4$: week^0, week^1, week^2, week^3, week^4. Cg: C. geophilum. a. showing BAARN grows faster than 1.278 under -0.6 MPa; b. showing BAARN has the same growth speed as 1.278 under -2 MPa; c. showing 1.278 grows faster than BAARN under-5 MPa. [SOURCE: Page_44]

Strains 1.278 and 1.58, respectively, exhibited a slow growth rate, whereas BAARN and 1.093 demonstrated rapid growth in the absence of PEG. Figure 4 illustrates that isolate BAARN exhibited a higher growth rate than isolate 1.093 in the absence of PEG, whereas isolate 1.093 displayed a higher growth rate than BAARN under -5 MPa conditions. Furthermore, at -5 MPa, strain 1.093 exhibited the highest growth rate among all isolates. Because of its rapid growth and high PEG tolerance, this isolate (1.093) was selected for subsequent experiments. [SOURCE: Page_44]

Osmotic stress induced by PEG induced changes in gene expression in C. geophilum 1.093 mycelia after 6, 24, and 48 h. [SOURCE: Page_44]

35 [SOURCE: Page_44]

The transfer of C. geophilum 1.093 mycelium from Cg control medium to PEG8000 supplemented medium to simulate drought stress (primarily osmotic stress) elicited significant gene expression changes as early as 6h after PEG treatment. Principal component analysis (Figure 5) of the overall gene expression in all samples differentiated on the first axis of the PEG-treated samples at 6h, 24h and 48h (T6S, T24S, T48S) from all control samples (T$_0$, T6C, T24C, T48C). The most substantial changes, with 643 (4.4%) upregulated and 394 (2.7%) downregulated transcripts, occurred as early as 6h after PEG treatment (Figure 5). Of the differentially expressed genes (DEGs) at 6h, only approximately one-third were also significantly regulated after 24h and 48h of PEG treatment, while the majority of the 24h and 48h DEGs were also regulated at a minimum of one of the other two time points (Figure 5). The ten most highly upregulated DEGs at 6h include stress-related transcripts, such as glutathione S-transferase (Cenge^3_673422) and heat shock protein (Cenge^3_285764). At 24h and 48h, genes encoding a stress-related RTA$_1$-like protein (Cenge^3_629648), MFS transporter (Cenge^3_531099), carbonic anhydrase (Cenge^3_611738), and oxidoreductase activity (Cenge^3_586501, Cenge^3_560674) were among the most induced genes. Many of the most highly upregulated transcripts were induced at all three time points following PEG treatment (Figure 5). At 6h after PEG treatment the DEGs included many genes known to be involved in cellular osmoregulation and homeostasis. Induced are genes coding for the C. geophilum homolog of the yeast HOG$_1$ stress activated protein kinase, part of the high osmolarity   glycerol   (HOG)      pathway    (Cenge^3_725216),      glycerol-3-phosphate dehydrogenase (Cenge^3_ 656861) involved in glycerol synthesis as well as genes linked to the sorbitol pathway (Supplementary Table 1, Supplementary Figure 2). GO enrichment analysis (Supplementary Table 2) of the biological processes of the upregulated DEGs identified "transport" as the most significant function at all three time points. Furthermore, the GO category "response to toxic substances" is enriched at 6h, "fatty acid biosynthetic process" at 24h, and "N-acetylglucosamine metabolic process" at 48h after PEG-treatment (Supplementary Table 2). GO enrichment analysis of the downregulated DEGs highlighted DNA-related categories (DNA replication, DNA recombination, DNA damage response, NTP biosynthesis, meiosis, and chromosome condensation) and translation (Supplementary [SOURCE: Page_45]

36 [SOURCE: Page_45]

Table 2). At all three time points, the upregulated transcripts enriched in KOG terms were secondary metabolite biosynthesis (KOG0023/1399), cell wall/membrane/envelope biogenesis (KOG4177), energy production (KOG1198/1575) and defense mechanisms (KOG1502, Supplementary Figure 3). In addition, the KEGG terms “oxidoreductases”, “alcoholdehydrogenases” and “with NAD+ or NADP+ as acceptor” were identified as enriched for the up-regulated transcripts in PEG-treated samples (Supplementary Figure 4). Approximately 10% of the DEGs at all three time points coded for putative transporters, with 13% of transporter genes being induced 6h after PEG treatment (Supplementary Figure 5, Supplementary Figure 6). At 6h, 32 of the DEGs coded for MFS transporters, including certain osmotic stress response-related transporters such as glycerol-, sugar/inositol-, or sugar/polyol-transporters. Many of these genes were up- regulated after 24 and 48h. Genes encoding several Amino Acid-Polyamine- Organocation (APC) family transporters, including GABA-specific permeases, have been shown to be induced by hyperosmotic stress. Furthermore, several ABC transporters, Voltage-gated K+ Channels, auxin-related transporters, Pore-forming NADPH-dependent 1-Acyldihydroxyacetone Phosphate Reductase (Ayr^1) family transporters, sulfate transporters, and aquaporins were induced 6h after PEG treatment. Approximately 25% of the down-regulated DEGs at 6h are associated with the KOG group “Information storage and processing” and in particular the KOG classes "Chromatin structure and dynamics," "Replication, recombination and repair," "RNA processing and modification," "Transcription" as well as "Translation, ribosomal structure and biogenesis" (Supplementary Figure 6), all DNA and translation related categories previously identified by the GO enrichment analysis. C. geophilum mycelium is highly melanized and its genome encodes multiple pathways for the synthesis of this black pigment. Supplementary Table 2 shows that all three DHN-melanin, DOPA-melanin, and L-tyrosine degradation pathways were downregulated after 6h of PEG treatment. At 48h after PEG-treatment, the gene expression of the enzymes involved in these pathways was comparable to the control and even slightly induced in the DHN-melanin pathway, potentially indicating that C. geophilum mycelium has adapted to PEG-induced osmotic stress and initiates growth [SOURCE: Page_46]

37 [SOURCE: Page_46]

and secondary metabolite production again. Up to 50% of the DEGs at the three time points were of unknown function, raising the question of whether the regulated genes after PEG treatment are conserved among fungi or whether some of them are C. geophilum-specific. Supplementary Figure 7 shows that the majority of DEGs at 6h and 48h share high homology with genes in the genomes of the selected fungi, indicating that they are not C.geophilum specific. Only appeoximately 11% of the 6h up-regulated transcripts (73/643) and 17% of the downregulated transcripts (66/394) showed no similarity with genes in the genomes of the selected fungi, with the majority having unknown functions. [SOURCE: Page_47]

C. geophilum 1.093 mycelium recovered rapidly from the PEG treatment after transfer to new medium. An important feature of drought tolerance is the ability to recover rapidly after cessation of stress and to resume normal metabolism and growth. At 24 h after transferring C. geophilum 1.093 mycelium from PEG-supplemented medium back into Cg control medium, only 52 DEGs were identified, whereas 327 transcripts were upregulated and 214 were downregulated after 48 h of recovery (Figure 5). PCA showed that the 24-hour and 48-hour recovery samples clustered with all other control samples without PEG treatment, indicating an overall similar gene expression profile (Figure 5A). Figure 6A presents a simplified heatmap comprising 1833 DEGs regulated in at least one of the PEG treatment time points and during the recovery time points. Upregulated DEGs compared to the respective controls were assigned a value of 1, downregulated DEGs a value of -1, and DEGs without significant regulation a value of 0. The heatmap (Figure 6 A) shows that the majority of PEG-stress-regulated DEGs were no longer differentially regulated after 24 and 48 h of recovery in fresh medium without PEG when compared to the mycelium control that was not PEG-treated previously. Only a small subset of DEGs at 24 and 48 h after recovery were also PEG stress-regulated and remained up- or downregulated after transfer to the new medium (Figure 6A). Figure 6B in Figure 6 depicts the relative gene expression values for the DEGs at 24 h and 48 h after transfer to new medium for both the samples that had been previously treated with PEG (SR) and the control samples without PEG (CR). Notably, the [SOURCE: Page_47]

38 [SOURCE: Page_47]

transcripts with higher and lower gene expression at 48 h recovery compared to the respective controls exhibited similar gene expression at 24 h for both recovery and control samples, suggesting that the samples that had been previously PEG-treated previously maintained gene expression in an activated or repressed state for a longer duration compared to the control samples. GO enrichment analysis identified "protein phosphorylation",   "regulation    of   DNA-templated      transcription",   and   "signal transduction" for the upregulated transcripts as putative functions, indicating an overall resumption of cellular activities and potentially enhanced growth of the recovering mycelium compared to the control. [SOURCE: Page_48]

Gene co-expression network analysis WGCNA was conducted using 1833 DEGs regulated in at least one of the treatments and time points. Eight modules were identified (Figure 7, upper panel). Transcripts associated with PEG treatment at 6h, 24h, and 48h clustered in the turquoise, blue, and red modules. The pink and brown modules comprised DEGs related to the 24h and 48h recovery from PEG treatment, as well as DEGs associated with the 24h control after transfer to new medium. The remaining control samples were linked to the green module (6h control), in addition to the black and yellow modules (24h control, 48h control, and 48h recovery control). WGCNA figure B illustrates the overall relationship between the modules in a network, closely associating the regulated DEGs from PEG- treated samples at 6, 24, and 48h (turquoise, blue, and red). The heatmap (WGCNA, lower right) emphasizes the co-regulation of transcripts within these modules. Regulation was primarily observed after 6h for the turquoise module, at 6h and 24h for the blue module, and at all three time points for the red module. GO enrichment analysis (Supplementary Table 3) of the biological processes identified "transport," "arginine catabolic process," and notably "carbohydrate transport" and "sulfate transport" for the turquoise module (6h), "catechol-containing compound metabolic process," DNA methylation, transport, glyoxylate cycle, and coenzyme A metabolic process for the blue module (6 and 24h), as well as fatty acid biosynthetic process, glycerol-3-phosphate metabolic/catabolic process, N-acetylglucosamine metabolic process, transport, gluconeogenesis, and polyamine biosynthetic process for the red module (all three timepoints). [SOURCE: Page_48]

39 [SOURCE: Page_48]

Discussion Research has demonstrated that C. geophilum exhibits resilience to drought conditions and is frequently observed in soil environments characterized by limited water availability [38]. Multiple investigations examining fungal communities in soil have revealed that the abundance of C. geophilum ECM and its external mycelial network increases under water-stressed conditions, often attaining higher levels during summer in natural settings [37,38]. Drought tolerance has been further corroborated through laboratory experiments using media with adjusted osmotic properties [39,12], assessments of cellular damage following dehydration [40], and measurements of respiration under water-limited conditions [14]. The degree of tolerance varies among isolates from diverse geographical locations [12,14]. However, these findings, the underlying molecular mechanisms that enable this species to thrive under water stress remain largely unknown. It is hypothesized that multiple factors contribute to this characteristic, including the accumulation of compatible osmolytes (such as polyols), heat shock proteins, hydrophobic proteins, and melanin pigment in cell walls. Although previous studies have investigated C. geophilum tolerance to osmotic stress at the transcriptome level [20], in this study the capacity for rapid recovery from drought stress was evaluated, by measuring growth speeds of each isolate in each water potential levels, which constitutes another critical component of stress management. Polyethylene glycol (PEG) was used in this study to alter the osmotic pressure of growth medium, which is frequently employed as a proxy to simulate drought stress. While drought stress encompasses multiple factors, including changes in soil physical properties that PEG cannot replicate, osmotic stress remains the primary focus when evaluating drought tolerance in ectomycorrhizal (ECM) fungi [12,22], demonstrating that mild drought stress (-0.15MPa) can stimulate their growth. This study was initiated by identifying a C. geophilum strain known for its rapid proliferation and resilience to increased osmotic pressure, particularly in response to elevated PEG concentrations. The study's primary objectives were to examine: (1) the swiftness of gene expression modifications in the isolate when exposed to PEG-induced drought stress; (2) the particular metabolic pathways and cellular functions that are regulated in PEG-treated mycelia compared to their untreated counterparts; and (3) the [SOURCE: Page_52]

43 [SOURCE: Page_52]

characteristics and dynamics of gene regulation for differentially expressed genes upon transitioning from PEG-containing to PEG-free environments. Our selection of a drought-tolerant C. geophilum strain involved both fast- and slow- growing isolates. Isolates from both drought-prone and wet environments were selected to compare their drought tolerance, revealing that even isolates from wetter sites exhibited potential for drought tolerance. In line with Coleman et al. [12], we observed that some isolates performed best in the control medium without PEG, whereas others thrived under water potentials of -2 to -3 MPa. In this study, BAARN belonged to the first group, whereas 1.093 and 1.278 were part of the second group, with 1.58, showing slow growth regardless of water availability. We confirmed the growth differences between the fast- and slow-growing isolates. Morel et al. [42] delineated intracellular detoxification processes in fungi, encompassing intracellular attack, conjugation, excretion, and storage, including for example, cytochrome P450 and glutathione transferase. Under PEG-induced osmotic stress, numerous genes from isolate 1.093 were upregulated as early as 6 h post- treatment. This included genes encoding cytochrome P450, glutathione transferase (among the most highly regulated transcripts at all three time points), and heavy metal transport/detoxification    protein/transporters,    corroborating     that    intracellular detoxification represents an early response to PEG-induced stress and likely constitutes one of the pathways involved in strain tolerance to osmotic stress. Furthermore, similar to certain model organisms such as Saccharomyces and Aspergillus, 1.093 responds to osmotic shock through changes in the osmolarity glycerol (HOG) pathway, cell wall remodeling, and osmolyte production [43–46]. Genes involved in glycerol and sorbitol biosynthesis pathways, as well as those related to glycerol, inositol, and polyol transport are also involved in 1.093 response to osmotic stress (Supplementary Figure 2). This response was less pronounced than that observed with mannitol, a well-established carbon storage pond for C. geophilum [16], and trehalose, an osmotically active compounds previously reported for other fungi, for which we did not identify induced genes in our experiment. While Li et al. [19] focused on the differences between tolerant and sensitive strains at a single time point, i.e., 24h after PEG treatment, our results revealed distinct gene expression patterns over different time points, suggesting a temporal dynamic in [SOURCE: Page_53]

44 [SOURCE: Page_53]

gene regulation rather than differences between isolates alone. WGCNA clustered PEG- treated samples into three modules (turquoise, blue, and red) with slightly different gene expression patterns. In the turquoise module, genes showed peak of expression at 6h including many transporters and genes involved in oxidoreduction. The blue module contains more genes with high expression levels maintained at 24h in addition to 6h, like as heat shock proteins or cytochrome P450, while in the red module gene up- regulation was high at all three time points, like as for the stress-related RTA$_1$-like protein. The highest number of DEGs compared to the control mycelium was detected as early as 6h after PEG-treatment, indicating the importance of following the response over several timepoints. Peter et al. [21] identified two aquaporin genes upregulated in ectomycorrhizal root tips and one aquaporin upregulated in mycelium exposed to agar drying as a proxy for drought stress. In our study, one of the ECM-induced aquaporins, Cenge^3_ 647346, was significantly upregulated at 6h, while the drought-stress induced aquaporin from Peter et al., Cenge^3_ 604158, was not significantly upregulated during stress and downregulated after 48h recovery. This observation suggests that aquaporins are likely less effective in addressing PEG-induced osmotic stress and in regulating water shortages during drought stress. The aforementioned finding is also applicable to another suggested component of the drought stress response in C. geophilum: the accumulation of melanin in cell walls. This accumulation also occurs in sclerotia, which can persist under various stress conditions for extended periods [47, 48]. In our experimental study, all three melanin synthesis pathways [49] were downregulated after 6 h of PEG treatment, suggesting that melanin synthesis is not essential for managing osmotic stress. Notably, 48 h following PEG treatment, the gene expression levels of the enzymes involved in these pathways returned to control levels, potentially indicating that C. geophilum mycelium has successfully adapted to PEG-induced osmotic stress and can resume growth and secondary metabolite production. Another important feature of tolerance is the ability to recover quickly after stress has disappeared. This is what we observed for the 1.093 mycelia after the end of PEG treatment. After 24h of recovery, the overall gene expression was similar to that of the control mycelium, after 48h of recovery, genes related to energy consumption and [SOURCE: Page_54]

45 [SOURCE: Page_54]

protein synthesis were induced compared to the control, indicating a renewed growth of 1,093. The results of this study indicated that the tolerance of C. geophilum 1.093 to PEG- induced osmotic stress was achieved at the transcriptome level through a combination of rapid induction of classical detoxification and osmoregulation pathways (Figure 8), encompassing transport and rapid recovery via the swift return of gene expression to the control level. The majority of genes and pathways utilized by C. geophilum for this response are conserved among fungi and are not C. geophilum-specific; only 11% of the upregulated transcripts at 6h exhibited no homology with genes in related fungal genomes. The importance and functions of these unique and conserved genes need transformation system to study and prove. [SOURCE: Page_55]

Fig. 8 Response of C.geophilum 1.093 to PEG-induced stress Summary of gene expression changes in hyphae of C.geophilum 1.093 in response to osmotic stress. Arrows: up and down regulated gene. Blue line means not significantly regulated. Numbers is the protein ID of each gene. Yellow color phase is the membrane and flesh color phase is the cell wall. (By FigDraw tool (http://www.figdraw.com)) [SOURCE: Page_55]

Supplementary Figure 5 DEGs coding for putative transporters The number of up- and down-regulated transcripts coding for transporters are given for the different time points (T6 6h, T24 24h, T48 48h) and treatments (R recovery). Genes coding for transporters were classified using the transporter classification database (TCDB; https://www.tcdb.org/). The percentage of regulated transporter genes among the total number of regulated genes is given in addition. 57 [SOURCE: Page_66]

Supplementary Figure 6 Functional classification of regulated transcripts at 6, 24 and 48h. UP up-regulated, DOWN down-regulated. SSP small secreted proteins. [SOURCE: Page_67]

58 [SOURCE: Page_67]

59 [SOURCE: Page_68]

Supplementary Figure 7 Presence of C. geophilum PEG-regulated genes in other fungal genomes. The heatmap depicts a double hierarchical clustering of PEG-regulated C. geophilum genes at 6h and 48h (rows, fold change >5; FDR corrected P<0.05). PEG stress-regulated genes were blasted (BLASTP) against the indicated genomes to identify homologues. Homologues are colored from yellow to red depending on the percentage of similarity. On the left of the heatmap, clusters are highlighted by branches of the same color. The percentages of putative functional categories are given for each cluster as bargrams, and the percentages of genes in each cluster are shown. Glost^2: Glonium stellatum; Leppa^1: Lepidopterella palustris; Lopmy^1: Lophium mytilinum; Mytre^1: Mytilinidion resinicola; Rhyru^1: Rhytidhysteron rufulum; Hyspu^1: Hysterium pulicare; Linin^1: Lindgomyces ingoldianus; Cloaq^1: Clohesyomyces aquaticus; Delco^1: Delitschia confertaspora; [SOURCE: Page_69]
