Supplementary Table 3 GO enrichment analysis (biological process, Fisher p- value<0.05)) for the different timepoints after PEG treatment (upper pannel) and the WGCNA modules (lower pannel). GO terms with p-value <0.01 are highlighted in bold. [SOURCE: Page_73]

64 [SOURCE: Page_73]

Chapter 3 [SOURCE: Page_74]

Effects of a controlled Cenococcum geophilum [SOURCE: Page_74]

inoculation on the ectomycorrhizal community of [SOURCE: Page_74]

beech (Fagus sylvatica L.) under drought conditions [SOURCE: Page_74]

Effects of a controlled Cenococcum geophilum inoculation on the ectomycorrhizal community of beech (Fagus sylvatica L.) under drought conditions [SOURCE: Page_75]

Abstract Ongoing climate change has led to more frequent soil droughts in forest ecosystems. Numerous beech diebacks have been reported following several years of unusually low precipitation in France and Switzerland. Consequently, the development of forest management strategies to enhance the drought resistance of forest trees has become increasingly critical. Ectomycorrhizal (ECM) fungi establish mutualistic associations with the roots of forest trees. These mutualistic fungi increase the root uptake area and facilitate the absorption of water and nutrients from the soil. The ECM fungus Cenococcum geophilum is cosmopolitan and prevalent in ecosystems under abiotic environmental stress, and it often shows greater resilience to drought stress than other ECM fungi. Thus, this beneficial symbiont is a suitable candidate for evaluating the capacity of ECM fungi to enhance drought adaptation in trees. The objective of this study was to assess whether controlled mycorrhiza formation by C. geophilum could enhance the protection of beech plantlets already mycorrhized by a natural soil microbiome from drought stress. A pilot inoculation experiment was conducted in a greenhouse using a drought-tolerant isolate of C. geophilum (Chapter 2) and European beech seedlings. Drought stress was induced by decreasing the soil humidity in pots containing inoculated plantlets. After a few months of experimental drought, the ECM morphotypes were characterized, and their colonization rates were quantified. The results of this experiment demonstrated that isolate 1.093 of C. geophilum could form ECM with beech plantlets. However, in our experimental system, no positive effect of controlled inoculation with Cg$_1$.093 on the survival rate of European beech plantlets was observed following the application of drought stress. [SOURCE: Page_75]

Introduction Forests play a crucial role in water and carbon cycling. They provide habitats for diverse life forms and offer ecological and economic services to humans. Recently, forests have experienced more frequent soil droughts and heat waves, resulting in widespread forest diebacks (Hammond, 2022). The frequency and duration of dry extremes have increased in most regions of the world and this trend is exacerbated [SOURCE: Page_75]

66 [SOURCE: Page_75]

(Petrova, 2024). In Europe, forest mortality is consistently associated with drought (Senf, 2020). European beech (Fagus sylvatica) is the dominant timber tree species in the natural forests of Europe (Leuschner 2020). It is an economically and ecologically important species. With several unusual and combined heatwaves and droughts, severe growth reduction, early leaf senescence, leaf browning, and diebacks of beech trees have been reported across Central Europe since 2018. These observations suggest that this species is susceptible to climate change (Rukh et al. 2023). In Europe, according to validated models, beech growth has declined across large regions of its distribution in recent decades, and the reduction in growth is projected to range from −20% to more than −50% by 2090 (Martinez del Castillo et al.,2022). Consequently, developing forest management approaches to enhance the drought resistance of beech trees has become increasingly critical. [SOURCE: Page_76]

ECM fungi have been recognized as potential candidates for enhancing the drought tolerance of forest trees. Under drought conditions, they can facilitate the host plants to acquire more nutrients than non-mycorrhizal plants (Lehto & Zwiazek, 2011). Cosme (2023) employed a phylogenetic comparative method utilizing data from 1,638 extant species globally distributed to characterize the evolution of mycorrhizal strategies and drought adaptation. The detected correlated evolution revealed that gains and losses of drought tolerance occurred at accelerated rates in lineages with ecto- or ericoid mycorrhizas, which were on average approximately 15 and 300 times faster than in lineages with arbuscular mycorrhizal and naked root (non-mycorrhizal alone or with facultative arbuscular mycorrhizal) strategies, respectively, suggesting that mycorrhizas play a crucial facilitator role in the evolutionary processes of plant adaptation to critical changes in water availability across global climates. Danielsen and Polle (2014) observed that K and Mg increased exclusively in the leaves of poplar interacting with - Paxillus involutus under drought conditions. Li et al. (2021) investigated the Ca availability and Ca uptake of Quercus acutissima under drought conditions and found that compared to non-ECM (NM) treatment, the ECM fungus Xerocomus chrysenteron enabled Quercus acutissima to acquire more Ca under drought conditions. It appears that ECM fungi enhance the mechanisms that mitigate drought. A similar result was observed for Suillus luteus-P. sylvestris var. mongolica symbiosis (Yin et al., 2020). Some ECM fungi have demonstrated efficacy under moderate drought conditions. [SOURCE: Page_76]

67 [SOURCE: Page_76]

Nilsen et al. (1998) found that moderate drought stress and nitrogen concentration result in higher colonization rates for C. geophilum, while other ECM fungi were not significantly influenced by drought. Pena et al. (2013) that trees colonized by C. geophilum, Tomentella punicea or Tuber sp. exhibited increased N uptake under drought conditions compared with NM plants. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Some compounds of ECM fungi play important roles when subjected to drought. Shi et al. (2002) found drought stress caused the accumulation of sucrose, glucose and fructose, and of fungus-derived compounds (mannitol and arabitol) in ectomycorrhizal roots. The accumulation of sugar alcohols, which are known to counteract drought stress, is species-specific. Fernandez and Koide (2013) investigated the function of melanin in Cg plants under drought stress. They used the melanin biosynthesis inhibitor tricyclazole to inhibit melanin production. Melanin inhibition only had negative effects on growth when C. geophilum isolates were subjected to osmotic and desiccation stress (-1.7 MPa). [SOURCE: Page_77]

ECM fungi can influence the physiology of host plants under drought stress. Sebastiana et al. (2019) investigated Pisolithus tinctorius- oak symbiosis and found improved drought tolerance of this system could be related to leaf membrane lipid metabolism, such as fatty acid content and composition. Calvo-Polanco et al. (2019) showed that the increased drought resistance of Laccaria bicolor-poplar symbiosis is regulated by the expression of plant aquaporins and the ABA/cytokinin status. Herzog et al. (2012) studied exoenzyme profiles of C. geophilum associated with Quercus robur, Q. petraea and Q. pubescens under drought stress. The results showed that the exoenzyme activities of C. geophilum on Q. robur and Q. petraea (but not Q. pubescens) significantly increased under the elevated air temperature and drought combined treatment. [SOURCE: Page_77]

ECM fungi form the mantle surrounding the root tips to cover and protect them from drying out. Some ECM fungi, such as C. geophilum, are drought-tolerant and abundant in water-stressed habitats (Pigott, 1982; McCormack et al., 2017). The potential respiration activity of C. geophilum ECM is significantly less altered than that of other ECM fungi (Jany et al. 2003). Some ECM fungi, such as many lichen-forming ascomycetes, have thick and gelatinous cell walls that maintain vitality under drought [SOURCE: Page_77]

68 [SOURCE: Page_77]

stress. C. geophilumhyphae enlarged and became gelatinous when they are in contact with water, thus storing water and keeping the host root moist longer (Di Pietro 2007). [SOURCE: Page_78]

ECM inoculation is a potential method for plantation and forest restoration. Strawsine et al. (2024) studied whether pioneer dual-mycorrhizal hosts, Leptospermum scoparium, could facilitate the establishment of Nothofagus by providing an ECM inoculum. Metabarcoding analysis of the roots of monocultured and mixed pairs of cultured Nothofagus and Leptospermum seedlings indicated the potential of Leptospermum to promote the establishment of Nothofagus in restoration planting. Extreme drought events occurred during the experiment. Their results showed that the survival of Nothofagus seedlings was significantly improved by higher ECM root colonization (including Cenococcum sp., but mainly affected by Laccaria sp.) levels, suggesting that ECM fungi have the potential to help their hosts adapt to extreme drought stress. De Quesada et al. (2024) evaluated the transpiration rate of Pinus sylvestris seedlings under drought stress in greenhouse conditions by exposing seedlings to 10 ECM fungal species and three natural soil inoculums to study whether some ECM fungi increase the drought tolerance of nursery-raised seedlings. Their results showed that exposing seedlings to ECM fungi or soil inoculum had a positive effect on transpiration rate during the driest and recovery phases, suggesting that ECM fungi have the potential to enhance seedling drought tolerance in nursery plant production. A similar result was observed by Aryal et al. (2020) in a greenhouse and Mateus et al. (2024) in an in vitro system. In a greenhouse inoculation experiment under three months of drought treatment, Suillus luteus increased the colonization rates of Pinus sylvestris var. mongolica seedlings, and improved soil conditions (Yin et al., 2018). Taniguchi et al. (2021) inoculated Pinus tabulaeformis seedlings (a drought tolerant plant species) with three different kinds of soil: no additional adding soil (NS), soil sterilized with fungicide, and ECM-containing nontreated plantation soil (NPS) collected from a nearby P. tabulaeformis plantation in the Kubuqi Desert combining with “straw checkerboard barrier afforestation method” and sequenced the ECM fungi community of seedlings with different treatments. After two years, P. tabulaeformis survival did not differ among the three soil treatments; ECM colonization rate, total plant dry weight, leaf total nitrogen per seedling, and water use in the NPS treatment were much higher than those in the NS treatment. Structural equation modeling showed that soil [SOURCE: Page_78]

69 [SOURCE: Page_78]

inoculation improved nitrogen uptake and water use, and increased plant growth by enhancing the ECM colonization of P. tabulaeformis. Suillus, Peziza, Geopora, Tuber, and Tomentella were the dominant genera in the NPS treatment group. These results indicate that soil inoculation with ECM fungi aids in afforestation with ECM plants for the restoration of drylands. [SOURCE: Page_79]

Although C. geophilum is widely recognized as a drought-tolerant symbiont, this assertion has recently been debated. A study utilizing Pinus seedlings colonized by C. geophilum and subjected to drought stress showed that the drought resistance of ECM plantlets was not directly correlated with that of C. geophilum isolates cultivated in liquid medium (Zhang et al., 2024). Xie et al. (2024a) used inoculated Quercus mongolica and Tilia amurensis to investigate the response of ECM fungal communities and their exploration types under drought conditions in a pot system. The relative abundance of C. geophilum in both hosts decreased. Nickel et al. (2017) examined the changes in ECM fungal community diversity of European beech and Norway spruce forests under drought conditions by utilizing retractable roofs to exclude rain for three years. The results indicated that the abundance of C. geophilum decreased irrespective of depth, year, or host. Pellitier et al. (2024) investigated ECM-fungal communities of Populus trichocarpa roots which distributed across a precipitation gradient in the Pacific   Northwest    USA     using   taxonomic     (metabarcoding)     and   functional (metagenomics) approaches. Their results showed fungal communities in dry soils contain distinct aquaporin and hydrophobin gene sequences, and genes involved in fungal drought stress tolerance and fungal-mediated plant water uptake (including genes of melanin synthesis, hydrophobins, aquaporins, trehalose synthases, and other gene families) were not the most abundant in drier soils. Nilsen et al. (1998) investigated the effects of N addition and drought on ECM of Norway spruce trees in an outdoor pot lysimeter study. Their results showed that all mycorrhizal types were influenced by drought, but only C. geophilum was significant decreased. To contribute to the ongoing debate regarding the role of C. geophilum in drought stress mitigation, we inoculated beech seedlings with the drought-tolerant isolate Cg 1.093, as described in Chapter 2. Our hypotheses were: 1) inoculation with Cg 1.093 can facilitate beech seedling survival during extreme drought events. If not, 2) Cg$_1$.093 inoculation cannot mitigate beech seedling death, but Cg$_1$.093 mycelia can survive under extreme drought [SOURCE: Page_79]

70 [SOURCE: Page_79]

conditions. [SOURCE: Page_80]

Methods and materials Plant and fungal materials [SOURCE: Page_80]

This study was conducted in a greenhouse at room T°C at INRAE-Nancy. Fans are used to decrease T°C in summer, decreasing the greenhouse temperature by approximately 5 °C compared to the outdoor T°C. The beech seedlings were provided by Dr. Aurelie Deveau and Dr. Stéphane Uroz. In Spring 2022, F. sylvatica seedlings were collected from the INRAE experimental site in the Montiers-sur-Saulx forest (France). The ecosystem research site of Montiers is located in Northeastern France in the Meuse [SOURCE: Page_80]

Department (48°31' 54″N, 5°16' 08'' E) and is managed by the BEF Research Unit [SOURCE: Page_80]

(INRAE Grand Est – Nancy). Seedlings were sampled at the cotyledon stage. They were then planted in 3.5-L cylindrical plastic pots filled with natural soil, a type of clay that was sieved through a 1-cm mesh and grown under natural environmental conditions. One seedling was digged out, planted and grown in each pot. After one year of growth, they had well-developed root systems. Inoculation with C. geophilum mycelium was initiated in March 2023, using three layers of soil per pot. The top and bottom layers were composed of natural soil (3 kg/layer) from the Montiers experimental site. The middle layer was made of soil mixed homogeneously with C. geophilum 1.093 mycelium (5.5g/pot) from MMN liquid culture, peat, vermiculite ((V/V=1:4)) and 0.7 L MMN liquid medium (Marx, 1970) with 20 g/L glucose (for mycelium growing in the substrate, since no preculture for them) (total substrate weight: 500 g/pot) using a concrete mixer machine. A large proportion of the root system was located in the middle layer. White stones (500 g) were used to cover the surfaces of the pots (Figure 1). The drought-tolerant isolate Cg 1.093 was identified and characterized in Chapter 2. We assumed that the pre-existing soil fungal community was similar for all seedlings, as they grew in the same environment and the soil was well mixed before planting. Notable, some seedlings already have C. geophilum -like mycorrhizas before inoculating. [SOURCE: Page_80]

Experimental design [SOURCE: Page_80]

Three and a half months after inoculation, the mycorrhizal status of two randomly [SOURCE: Page_80]

71 [SOURCE: Page_80]

selected beech seedlings was assessed (Figure 5). C. geophilum – like ECM were found on the root systems. The drought treatments were then performed from July to August 2023 and lasted for 30 d. The soil humidity of the drought-treated and untreated control groups was 25% (total weight: 3340 g/pot) and 70% (total weight: 3950 g/pot), respectively, controlled by weighing the pots (Figure 2). The relationship between the total weight and soil humidity was calculated using 100% and 0% humidity. The 100% humidity soil was weighed from the soil that was submerged in water for half an hour and after which the unabsorbed water was removed. The 0%-humidity soil was placed in an oven (> 50 °C) for > 12 h. The pots were weighed and watered every day, except on weekends. The experiment started with 34 pots as follows: [SOURCE: Page_81]

– A: no drought treatment and no inoculation (8 pots). [SOURCE: Page_81]

–B: inoculated with Cg$_1$.093 and drought treatment (9 pots), [SOURCE: Page_81]

– C: no inoculation but without drought treatment (8 pots), [SOURCE: Page_81]

– D: no inoculation and drought treatment (9 pots), and [SOURCE: Page_81]

After three and a half months, 4 pots died naturally (didn’t spread their branches). 30 seedlings were survived (7, 7, 8, and 8 seedlings as mentioned above). After the experimental treatment, all seedlings from the drought treatment, but without inoculation, died due to an unusual heat wave in the summer (Figure 2). Only^12 pots of seedlings survived (4 pots for each of the remaining groups, Table 1) [SOURCE: Page_81]

Sampling and measurements [SOURCE: Page_81]

In August 2023, four pots (not transferred) from each group were selected based on their similarity, and the total fresh weight, shoot and root fresh weight, collar diameter, and height were recorded. Root systems with ECM were sampled randomly and fixed in ethanol for ECM identification and ECM rate assessment (by their phenotypes). Additional roots were collected and frozen at −80°C for future amplicon sequencing of the ECM fungal community. Owing to the high loss rate and the low remaining sample number, we opted not to perform this sequencing analysis for this pilot experiment. The ECM phenotype was identified using the DEEMY database (http://www.deemy.de/). [SOURCE: Page_81]

The Kolmogorof Smirnov test was conducted on the data of each group using R software to determine the distribution. All group data fit a normal distribution (p >0.05). [SOURCE: Page_81]

72 [SOURCE: Page_81]

Then the standard deviation for each group and overall Levine test were conducted by using R packages: “psych” (Revelle, 2024), and “car” (Fox and Weisberg, 2019) to test their homogeneity of variance. The standard deviations of each two groups were different, except for those of groups B and D (both p = 0.01). According to the results of the Levene test, all groups had similar variances (p > 0.1). Therefore, variance between different groups was analyzed by Student’s t-test (Table 2), and it was performed using Excel. [SOURCE: Page_82]

Results [SOURCE: Page_82]

C. geophilum – like and other ECM fungi, originally in the soil of Montiers experimental site, colonized on the roots of the lower soil layer and of the lower than second-class lateral roots (according to my observation). A relatively low colonization rate of C. geophilum was observed (approximately, < 15%, according to my observation). Colonized and non-colonized new root systems are quite different. Most of the new roots are first-class later roots, which help seedlings to have a better root system (supporting shoots fixing on the ground). They have a relatively larger diameter and are fleshier, which cannot be recognized as fine roots and are more frequently formed in spring (March to May). The colonized roots are fine roots and are more abundant in summer. Three main ECM morphotypes were identified in this study. According to our DEEMY database identification, these ECM were established by Lactarius, C. geophilum, and Tuber species (Figure 3). In a pre-test experiment carried out by Dr. Deveau (INRAE – Nancy) (which used same way, but no C. geophilum inoculation), ECM of Tuber spp. was found in both drought-treated and control seedlings. The ECM root tips of C. geophilum and Tuber were then counted, and their colonization rate was measured (Table 3). Since only colonized root systems were selected to count the colonization rate, the total mycorrhizal rates of the whole root system were much lower. For example, the highest colonized beech seedling, C26, had 67.3% colonized root tips, but only about less than 5% root tips were colonized in the whole root system (according to my observation). During the drought treatment, all trees in group D died. Therefore, they had more dead root tips and dead ECM. The other group (B) with drought treatment also had more dead roots than control group A (Figure^1 g and h, according to my observation and obviously). [SOURCE: Page_82]

73 [SOURCE: Page_82]

## 12.00% P = 0.04356 10.00%

Figure 4 C. geophilum colonization rates in the different groups X-axis showing the colonization rates of C. geophilum; Y-axis showing the treatment groups and the names of pots; The colonization rates of group A and B are significant different. +/- Cg means with/without C. geophilum 1.093 inoculation; +/- drought means with/without drought treatment [SOURCE: Page_89]

Figure 5 C. geophilum-like fungus is able to colonize beech seedlings Arrows ectomycorrhizas [SOURCE: Page_89]

80 [SOURCE: Page_89]

Drought treatment significantly reduced the colonization rate of C. geophilum (among the Cg-inoculation groups: p = 0.0435) and Tuber spp. (colonization rates variate from 0.41% to 0%), but a small number of C. geophilum ECM survived (Figure 4). Not all ECM root tips were affected equally by drought treatment. Two unknown ECM fungi dominated and increased their colonization rate under drought treatment: a yellow ECM fungus (Figure 3 a) in the C. geophilum inoculation group, and a black ECM fungus (Figure 3 f) in the control group. [SOURCE: Page_90]

Discussion In this study, we compared beech seedlings grown in pots containing natural soil or soil mixed with C. geophilum 1.093 and subjected them to drought treatment or control conditions. Our results indicate that drought significantly decreased the colonization of beech seedlings by C. geophilum, suggesting that this fungus is unable to form additional ECM root tips on beech roots under drought conditions. Groups C and D did not receive additional inoculation with C. geophilum 1.093 but contained C. geophilum isolates from natural soil. C. geophilum ECM were likely formed more by these ‘wild’ isolates, suggesting that they are more efficient than C. geophilum 1.093 to drought stress. Consequently, it would be valuable to isolate these locally adapted strains. Furthermore, C. geophilum 1.093 did not play a central role in the ECM-fungal community when the host experienced drought stress. Thus, in our experimental setup, strain 1.093 was unable to improve its host survival rate. This may be related to the short duration of the experiment. The results of our experiment do not support Hypothesis 1. However, we tested only one isolate in a greenhouse. Testing other isolates from different clades (Obase et al., 2017, also see Chapter 2) and inoculating roots under field conditions would contribute to a more comprehensive understanding of C. geophilum under drought stress. [SOURCE: Page_90]

The leaves and fine roots of beech trees are sensitive to drought stress (Leuschner, 2020). In the present study, most of the roots died rapidly after drought stress. One reason could be that the trees were in relatively small pots, and the beech – ECM symbiosis suffered double stress, drought, and heat (over 35 °C). Furthermore, under natural conditions, beech seedlings grow under their mother trees, which provides a shaded and less stressful environment. Light conditions may also co-affect the drought [SOURCE: Page_90]

81 [SOURCE: Page_90]

treatment, since the drought treatment in the experiment was conducted during summer. Pena et al. (2013) investigated the influence of ECM fungi (including C. geophilum) on the architecture of and N partitioning in young F. sylvatica plants in response to different light regimes and water deprivation. The results indicated that ECM fungi were able to benefit beech seedlings under mild drought, but the responses differed depending on light availability: shaded ECM plants showed a delay in the decrease of pre-dawn water potential. [SOURCE: Page_91]

Recently, several studies have shown that the exploration type of ECM fungi (Agerer, 2021) is closely linked to their colonization ability under drought stress. Long- exploring-type ECM fungi have more ECM with beech in the field under drought stress (Nickel et al., 2017). However, a potted oak seedling inoculation experiment showed that the long-exploring type of ECM fungi colonized fewer root tips under drought stress (Xie et al., 2024a) which has been shown related to carbon limitation (Xie et al., 2024b). Beech trees have higher root turnover rates (Nikolova et al., 2020). Therefore, the use of a long-exploration-type ECM fungus as a partner could be costly and detrimental. In this study, we found that the amount of contact type and short-distance type (Agerer, 2021) ECM (Lactarius, Cenococcum, and Tuber, probably linked to the inoculation source from natural soil) decreased. However, it is unknown whether these ECM fungi can recover rapidly. Unknown ECM fungi have been identified. According to their morphology, they are short – middle distance exploration types. This finding supports the energy limitation hypothesis (Xie et al., 2024b). However, both the second and the present study were conducted in pot systems and not in the forest. [SOURCE: Page_91]

It remains to be shown whether the root system, the rooting style, plays a role in the drought tolerance of beech. Danzberger et al. (2024) investigated the fungal community response of beech seedlings to drought, which were established from the same seed source and grown in three soils with different precipitation histories, but similar water retention properties. They found that seedlings grown in dry region soils with adapted fungal communities were the most affected by drought. And the result showed the drought responses of beech seedlings depend largely on their different rooting patterns and less on soil fungal communities that are adapted to long-term precipitation conditions. In our study, we used a relatively short inoculation period. However, in the inoculation period, the new roots were mainly structural roots (main root or first-class [SOURCE: Page_91]

82 [SOURCE: Page_91]

later root) instead of absorbing roots (second- or lower-class later roots) – fine roots. Therefore, it was probably difficult for C. geophilum to colonize the roots. For future experiments, we would suggest waiting for beech rooting and adding the inoculum until the beech seedlings start to form new fine roots. In addition, although the results showed high ectomycorrhizal fungal colonization for sampled root system (part of whole root system) in some treatment groups of beech seedlings, they still had a low colonization rate on the whole root system level. It seems that there is an age effect in the beech root systems. Although the beech tree is known as a shallow root system- forming species, at least for 27 years, the tree maintains its deep main roots to have a better structure (Providing support for shoots. Harley, 1940). As the trees aged, the long surface branches became increasingly important as absorbing organs, and at 80 years, shallow outgoing branches of the heart root comprised the greater part of the functional root system (Harley, 1940). Therefore, the seedlings had more main roots or first-class later roots than the fine roots. In this period, although some shallow roots can be colonized, the ectomycorrhizal fungi probably have difficulties in colonizing most of the beech roots because of the physical matter – depth of the soil. Some fungi cannot use nutrients from the mineral layer – deep layers (Morel et al., 2006). Therefore, it is important to consider the living layer, exploring type of ectomycorrhizal fungi and root structure of the host tree. [SOURCE: Page_92]

In the present study, we used a three-layer pot system to inoculate beech seedlings. C. geophilum mycelium was in the 0–5 cm layer, and peat vermiculite has a looser soil structure than natural soil (clay). However, during drought treatment, this layer works like a filter, making the whole pot system soil inhomogeneous and making the first and second layers dry out. It stopped the water transfer from the bottom back to the upper layers through the water potential gradients and caused water to enter the lower layers. Therefore, in future experiments, it would be better to use sterile natural soil to make the inoculum or to pre-inoculate the beech in a growth chamber before being transferred to the greenhouse. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Conclusions In our experimental setup, the C. geophilum isolate 1.093 failed to enhance ECM formation under drought conditions. Future studies should consider several factors: the [SOURCE: Page_92]

83 [SOURCE: Page_92]

ECM exploration type, the status of the root system, the capacity of different-aged beech hosts to form fine roots, the inoculation technique (utilizing sterilized natural soil for a more uniform inoculum or implementing a pre-inoculation phase after beech seedlings begin developing new fine roots), and environmental factors (seasonal changes, light conditions). [SOURCE: Page_93]

88 [SOURCE: Page_97]

Chapter 4 [SOURCE: Page_98]

Establishment of ectomycorrhizal associations between [SOURCE: Page_98]

ectomycorrhizal fungi, including Cenococcum [SOURCE: Page_98]

geophilum and beech (Fagus sylvatica L.) seedlings [SOURCE: Page_98]

using an in vitro experimental system [SOURCE: Page_98]

Establishment of ectomycorrhizal associations between ectomycorrhizal fungi, including Cenococcum geophilum and beech (Fagus sylvatica L.) seedlings using an in vitro experimental system [SOURCE: Page_99]

Abstract European beech (Fagus sylvatica L.) is an economically and ecologically important tree species in Europe. In recent decades, several in vitro experimental systems have been established to investigate the molecular mechanisms underlying the development and function of ectomycorrhizal symbiosis (ECM). However, no in vitro system has yet been developed to produce ECM using beech seedlings, plantlets, and ECM fungi, despite the existence of protocols for the in vitro micropropagation of beech cuttings. In this study, we examined various approaches to obtain in vitro cultures of beech from different explants, such as seeds and branches, from local forests. Additionally, we conducted preliminary tests to establish ECM symbiosis between the in vitro cuttings of beech lines and various ECM fungi. We successfully established an in vitro system for rooting F. sylvatica lines (FS-ap, FS-10-4), Fagus orentalis lines (FO-BS, FO Clone^3, FO Clone^30), obtained from Dr. Castro and Martínez (CSIC). We found that the beech line Fs-ap can form a mantle, but not a Hartig net, with Cenococcum geophilum 1.093 and 1.058, Laccaria bicolor S238N, and Lactarius subdulcis 3. C. geophilum 1.093 formed distinct endophytic structures in the root tips of beech Fs-ap. L. subdulcis 3 can establish a fungal mantle on roots of the beech line Fo-BS, but no Hartig net was observed. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Introduction European beech (Fagus sylvatica) is an important tree species in Europe. After several unusually dry years in France and Switzerland, many beech diebacks were reported. Climate change threatens the sustainability of forest ecosystems. The development of forest management approaches to improve the drought resistance of forest trees has become increasingly important (Antonucci et al., 2021). Ectomycorrhizal (ECM) fungi form mutualistic interactions with the roots of the forest trees. They increase the root uptake area within the soil and facilitate water and nutrient uptake from the soil (Smith et al., 2021). This beneficial symbiont is a good candidate to assess the ability of ECM [SOURCE: Page_99]

90 [SOURCE: Page_99]

fungi to enhance drought adaptation in trees. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Many ECM fungi, such as species from the genera Laccaria, Lactarius, Cenococcum, Amanita, and Russula, are known to form symbiotic associations with beech trees (Boeraeve, 2021). Some of these, such as Lactarius subdulcis and Lactarius blennius, are commonly found to be associated with beech trees (Eryssartier & Roux, 2017). Cenococcum geophilum establishes ECM associations with oak, but can form ectendomycorrhizas with beech (see Chapter 1). Both abundant and rare fungi colonize beech trees (Dietrich et al., 2022). Greenhouse experiments or field research on ECM fungi have used beech as a host material in natural soils. A series of early studies on beech ECM focused on carbohydrate metabolism. The fungus can absorb sugar from its host, trehalose and mannitol are synthesized by the fungus from glucose imported from their host. These reserve sugars are not readily available to the host (Lewis and Harley 1965 a,b,c). Anthony et al. (2022) studied both broadleaf and conifer forest (including European beech forest). Their results showed that beech tree growth was linked to mycorrhizal fungal composition and function across Europe. Audisio et al. (2024) used potted sapling pairs of European beech and North American Douglas-fir (Pseudotsuga menziesii) for 13CO$_2$ pulse-labeling. The 13C transferred from beech (donor) to either beech or Douglas-fir (recipient) was compared. The results showed that beech recipients shared more ECM species and had a higher 13C enrichment. However, little is known about the molecular mechanisms driving ECM development and metabolism in beech trees, mainly because of the lack of a simplified model. The development of an in vitro beech mycorrhizal system is important for closing this knowledge gap. Several model systems have been used to study ECM development at a molecular level. Among these, the Laccaria – Populus (Felten et al., 2009), Suillus – Pinus (Lofgren et al., 2024), Pisolithus – Eucalyptus (Burgess et al., 1996), and Piloderma-Quercus (Krüger, 2004) systems have been used to advance our understanding of ECM symbiosis. To the best of our knowledge, no in vitro system has yet been established to investigate ECM symbiosis in beech trees. [SOURCE: Page_100]

An in vitro micropropagation protocol for beech has been established previously (Chalupa, 1996). Vieitez et al. (1993) obtained F. sylvatica lines from the embryonic axes of seeds and cultured them on woody plant medium (WPM) supplemented with 6- benzyladenine (BA). Shoots and basal segments were transferred to WPM medium [SOURCE: Page_100]

91 [SOURCE: Page_100]

supplemented with 0.5 mg/L BA, 2, and 0.2 mg/L 1-Naphthaleneacetic Acid (NAA). Karin and Gerhard (1994) investigated the endogenous factors necessary for the micropropagation of mature F. sylvatica. The results indicated that the optimal time to obtain explants was February, and the most suitable explant source was apical buds > 20 mm from 1-year-old shoots. Cuenca and Vieitez (1999) examined the effects of carbon source and concentration on shoot multiplication in F. sylvatica and F. orientalis. 3-4% glucose was determined to be the optimal carbon source and concentration. The response of shoot cultures to different carbohydrate treatments appeared to be genotype- dependent to some extent. Cuenca et al. (2000) utilized previously obtained materials to assess the bud regeneration capacity. The highest regeneration potential was observed in apical internodes, whereas those distal to the apex were the least productive. The elongated shoots of adventitious origin demonstrated the ability to proliferate via axillary branching. The optimal bud induction medium was found to be WPM containing 30 g/L sucrose, 7 g/L agar, 2.9 µM IAA and 4.5 µM thidiazuron (TDZ). The shoot proliferation medium consisted of WPM supplemented with 2.2 µM BA, 9.1 µM zeatin, 2.9 µM IAA, 20 g/L glucose and 7 g/L agar. The optimal rooting medium was determined to be 1/2 WPM with 20 g/L sucrose without any auxin. [SOURCE: Page_101]

In the present study, we established in vitro lines of F. sylvatica and F. orientalis under laboratory conditions and conducted preliminary experiments to test the mycorrhizal ability of a set of ECM fungi, including Lactobacillus subdulcis strain 3, Cenococcum geophilum (strains 1.58 and 1.093), and Laccaria bicolor strain S238N. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Materials and methods Plant and fungal materials [SOURCE: Page_101]

We first attempted to obtain sterilized beech seedlings from seeds and explant materials from sterilized branches of beech seedlings in winter (Figure 1, test 1). Seeds were purchased from Vilmorin (FSY201 NORD-EST /France and FSY501 JURA /France, 2021) and pretreated by this company for gemination. The shells of seeds were removed, and the seeds were sterilized using 30% H2O$_2$ for 8 min and transferred to MMN medium to check the contamination to have different genetic backgrounds except in [SOURCE: Page_101]

92 [SOURCE: Page_101]

vitro beech plantlets. New shoots of Fagus sylvatica var. pendula (over 50 years), orientalis × sylvatica (over 50 years), orientalis (over 50 years), sylvatica (mixed: 30 - 40 years and less than 5 years), and var. tortuosa (approximately 20-30 years) were cut from trees growing in the INRAE arboretum (Champenoux, France). They were cut into 10-20 cm segments that were sterilized using copper oxychloride (purchased from Cultivers, sterilizing method described by Martínez et al., 2017), and cultured in plastic boxes filled with perlite and distilled water (Figure 2). The plastic boxes were tightly closed using tape. The culture conditions were as described by Cuenca et al. (2000) and light cycling by Felten et al. (2009). [SOURCE: Page_102]

Second, we obtained in vitro beech cultures. Fagus sylvatica lines (FS-ap, FS-10-4), Fagus orientalis lines (FO-BS, FO Clone^3, FO Clone^30), and their culturing protocols were obtained from Dr. Elena Corredoira Castro and Marí a Teresa Martínez, Consejo Superior de Investigaciones Científicas (CSIC), Spain (Vieitez et al., 1993; Cuenca and Vieitez, 1999; Cuenca et al., 2000). Briefly, cuttings were cultured in a proliferation medium (Cuenca et al., 2000). They were then rooted in a rooting medium (Figure 1, test 2). After rooting, only the FS-ap line was used for tissue culture, whereas lines FS- ap and FO-BS were used for synthesis. Petri dishes cannot provide enough space for beech growth, and tubes cannot provide enough surface area for mycelia and roots to contact each other. When using Petri dishes, in contrast to MS medium, WPM medium has more (approximately 50 ml, the volume is known after using the flasks) water at the bottom, which cannot be controlled by adding cotton rolls (up to six rolls cannot control all the water) and always causes heavy contamination. Therefore, we used Falcon® 225 cm²or 150 cm²(surface area) rectangular canted neck cell culture flasks with vented caps (Dutscher/France) for culturing and rooting the beech cuttings. Furthermore, we used TPP® Tissue Culture Flask with Vented Cap and re-closable Lid 150 cm² (Dutscher/France) for mycorrhiza development. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Third, we also attempted to obtain local in vitro cultures from young trees using the culturing protocol. One hundred branches were sampled from 20-year-old trees in the Amance State Forest, near the INRAE-Nancy Center. They were cut into 5-10 cm segments with the basal part of the leaves (Figure 1, test 3). They were then sterilized using 70% ethanol for 1-1.5 min and 5% bleach for 8 min. The culture conditions and protocol were the same as those used by our Spanish collaborators (see above). [SOURCE: Page_102]

93 [SOURCE: Page_102]

Starting cultures of strains 1.058 and 1.093 from C. geophilum, S238N from L. bicolor, and strain 3 from L. subdulcis were obtained from the INRAE-IAM Fungal Collection. The mycelium was transferred by cutting 2 cm diameter plugs (5 plugs/cellophane) and cultured on MMN medium supplemented with 20 g/L glucose. Two pieces of cellophane were placed between the medium and plugs. Before contact with the roots, the mycelium was cultured on cellophane for one month (Figure 3). The plugs were removed from the cellophane. Only the outer part was used for the inoculation. [SOURCE: Page_103]

Experimental design [SOURCE: Page_103]

The beech lines and fungal mycelium (Fs-ap with C. geophilum 1.058 and 1.093, L. bicolor S238N and L. subdulcis 3, and Fo-BS with L. subdulcis 3) were placed in contact and cultured using a sandwich system (Felten et al., 2009). Since the in vitro beech, the synthesis medium for beech seedlings was beech rooting medium. Furthermore, P20 medium (Felten et al., 2009) was tested as a synthetic medium for beech lines with C. geophilum 1.093 and 1.058. Beech seedlings were co-cultured with fungal mycelia for 2 months. Owing to the limited number of rooted plants, only one plant was tested for each combination. Finally, 5-10 root tips for each combination were fixed in paraformaldehyde for microscopy. Cross-sections for microscopy observations were obtained as described by Basso et al. (2020). [SOURCE: Page_103]

Results Propagating beech lines from seeds and branches [SOURCE: Page_107]

A low germination rate was observed for beech seeds following treatment with 5% bleach for 10 min and the majority of seeds remained contaminated (Figure 4). Attempts to sterilize beech seedlings from seeds were unsuccessful. [SOURCE: Page_107]

F. sylvatica exhibited the highest number of sprouted buds. In the absence of auxin and cytokinin, the beech branches developed new buds. The application of copper oxychloride proved insufficient for sterilizing beech branches. A significant proportion of beech branches was found to be contaminated. Furthermore, attempts to sterilize beech seedlings from branches of mature trees were unsuccessful. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Figure 5 Tissue culture of beech 98 [SOURCE: Page_107]

Tissue culture, micropropagation and rooting of beech in vitro lines (Figure 1, test 2) [SOURCE: Page_108]

All beech shoots grew well on the proliferation medium. Except for Fs-10-4, all lines produced roots in the rooting medium (Figure 5). The segments of in vitro cultures (cuttings) or the basal part of in vitro cultures (only tested Fs-ap) could form calluses using bud induction medium (Figure 5). With calli, cuttings grow better, but after 2-3 months, the callus became brownish and the leaves died (Figure 1, step 2). Beech cuttings had a callus at the basal part could occasionally form another bud (less than 1/50). Therefore, it was difficult to multiply the cultures using bud regeneration in our laboratory. Therefore, we tested whether calluses from the basal part of the culture (without any other shoots on the callus) could form new buds for regeneration. With all media used in published papers or protocols (Vieitez et al., 1993; Cuenca and Vieitez, 1999; Cuenca et al., 2000), the callus never formed a new bud – the beginning of new branches. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Establishing local beech in vitro line (Figure 1, test 3) [SOURCE: Page_108]

Given that callus formation did not result in new shoot development, an alternative protocol was investigated for the in vitro multiplication of beech shoots. Branches were collected from 20-year-old trees in the local forest. Unfortunately, these samples exhibited high contamination rates, and no callus formation was observed under our experimental growth conditions. Only two out of 100 sampled branches responded to the proliferation and rooting media by extending new branches from buds and developing without contamination. However, no root formation was observed in the rooting medium after three weeks (Figure 7). [SOURCE: Page_108]

In vitro beech ectomycorrhiza development [SOURCE: Page_108]

The P20 medium was determined to be unsuitable for the ECM formation between beech and C. geophilum strains 1.093 and 1.058. The roots ceased growth and exhibited browning (Fig. 8). At this stage, only a loose aggregation of hyphae – a pseudo-mantle – was observed on primary roots (Figure 8). The interaction between FS-ap and C. geophilum 1.058 was examined utilizing confocal microscopy on cross sections. Only [SOURCE: Page_108]

99 [SOURCE: Page_108]

the mantle was formed, and no Hartig net was observed (Figure 9). [SOURCE: Page_109]

The tissue culture flasks are good containers for both micropropagation (Figure 6 and 7) and mycorrhiza synthesis for beech (Figure 10-13). After 2 months of coculture, although no typical ectomycorrhiza were observed, the beech line Fs-ap grow differently with the four ectomycorrhizal fungi. The Lactarius subdulcis 3 – Fs-ap combination died (Figure 13). Laccaria bicolor S238N helped beech line Fs-ap grow much better than did C. geophilum 1.093 and 1.058 (much more green, Figure 10-12). [SOURCE: Page_109]

Cross sections of the root tips among 4 ectomycorrhizal fungi and 2 beech line combinations [SOURCE: Page_109]

No Hartig net was observed for any combination (Figure 14 and 15). However, all specimens exhibited hyphal aggregation around roots which may correspond to a pseudomantle. The structure of the root tips from beech Fs-ap with C. geophilum 1.093 differed from that of other combinations. The cross-section revealed that the mycelium penetrated the epidermis but did not form a Hartig net (Figure 14). It was observed that subsequent to penetration, the mycelium proliferated along the root. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Discussion All the seeds were contaminated or not germinated. It appears that beech seeds contain endophytes. Therefore, it is not possible to obtain in vitro seedlings from sterilized seeds. After 2 months, the cuttings cultured in the proliferation medium were transferred to the rooting medium to obtain roots. Without roots, the basal callus will affect the cutting and finally lose the cutting after 3 months. Compared with Fagus orentalis lines, the Fagus sylvatica line Fs-ap has a lower ability to form later roots (my observation). The main roots stopped growing until they touched the water at the bottom of the flasks and then started to have later roots. Therefore, the Fagus sylvatica lines had less absorption of fine roots. In natural soil, young Fagus sylvatica seedlings also show low ability of having more lateral roots for at least 27 years (Harley, 1940). No typical ECM structures were observed using either the two beech lines or our set of ECM fungi. Lactarius subdulcis 3 cannot colonize FS-ap and cannot benefit the growth of beech seedlings. Laccaria bicolor S238N can help Fs-ap grow well (much more greenish and no falling leaf). L. subdulcis 3 can help FO-SB grow well. More lines of Fagus sylvatica should be tested with these ECM fungi under in vitro conditions. Fs-ap formed a special structure with C. geophilum 1.093, and the root tips did not form a Hartig net but penetrated the root tips and grew along the root. According to our microscopic observations, C. geophilum 1.093 formed endophytic structures, which did not benefit beech growth as L. bicolor S238N did for Fs-ap. However, more replicates are required to confirm this hypothesis. [SOURCE: Page_120]

Conclusions New shoots can be readily obtained from in vitro cuttings; however, we were unable to generate new in vitro lines of F. sylvatica by using seeds or branches from 20-year-old trees. In contrast, we successfully propagated in vitro beech lines obtained from CSIC collaborators. These lines were rooted and used to establish symbiotic associations with a set of ECM fungi. We were unable to observe the ECM structures under our experimental conditions. Nevertheless, some of these interactions enhance the growth of beech plantlets. [SOURCE: Page_120]

111 [SOURCE: Page_120]

114 [SOURCE: Page_123]

Chapter 5 [SOURCE: Page_124]

Conclusions, Discussion and Perspectives [SOURCE: Page_124]

Conclusions In my thesis project, I investigated the mechanisms underlying drought tolerance, including adaptation to osmotic stress, of the ectomycorrhizal ascomycete Cenococcum geophilum at various levels using different methodological approaches, which can be summarized as follows: [SOURCE: Page_125]

•   Under sterile controlled culture conditions, a drought-tolerant isolate, C. geophilum 1.093, was identified (Chapter 2). Changes in gene expression during growth at high PEG concentrations were significantly and differently expressed, as early as 6h. It recovered after treatment according to its transcriptome. (Chapter 2). •   Under non-sterile greenhouse conditions, we found C. geophilum - like ectomycorrhizas, but we are not sure whether isolate 1.093 formed ectomycorrhizas with beech (Fagus sylvatica) roots while growing on natural forest soils in irrigated pots. And these ectomycorrhizas did not enhanced the survival rate of beech seedlings during growth under low soil water conditions (Chapter 3). •   An in vitro system using F. sylvatica and F. orientalis lines was successfully established (Chapter 4). Although C. geophilum 1.093 formed distinct endophytic structures in the beech root tips, it did not form the expected ectomycorrhizal Hartig net. In contrast, the ectomycorrhizal fungi Laccaria bicolor S238N and Lactarius subdulcis 3 were capable of forming a fungal mantle, although a Hartig net was not observed (Chapter 4). [SOURCE: Page_125]

Discussion and Perspectives The identification of the drought-tolerant isolate, C. geophilum 1.093, described in Chapter 2, was pivotal for subsequent studies. Although isolates 1.058 and 1.278 demonstrated slow growth under drought stress, their potential to survive suggests that they could also be tested in greenhouse experiments. Despite the ability of strain 1.093 to form ectomycorrhizal structures, its inability to improve beech seedling survival (Chapter 3) requires identification and characterization of other natural isolates that may offer better performance under the same conditions. Furthermore, our transcriptomic analysis using RNA sequencing revealed several pathways and gene candidates linked to drought tolerance (i.e., high PEG concentration), although many remain functionally unknown. Unfortunately, there is currently no transformation system for C. geophilum, which limits further functional genomic analyses of these differentially expressed genes. [SOURCE: Page_125]

In Chapter 3, water potential in pots hosting mycorrhizal beech seedlings was managed via automated watering and soil weighing; however, the variable root systems of young beech trees [SOURCE: Page_125]

116 [SOURCE: Page_125]

complicate the assessment of drought tolerance in these seedlings. The inclusion of xylem or leaf water potential measurements can provide a better understanding of the water status of these plants. The unscheduled heat stress experienced in the greenhouse due to inadequate fan cooling during summer coupled with drought stress further complicated our experimental settings and precluded the obtention of robust, replicated findings. Future experiments on ectomycorrhizal beech seedlings should be conducted under controlled temperature conditions, ideally indoors, during spring. [SOURCE: Page_126]

In Chapter 4, the inability of the tested beech lines to form mycorrhizas with the four selected ectomycorrhizal fungi highlighted the need for genotype-specific combinations. Future studies should involve pre-screened fungal-beech line combinations (same container but change medium to substrate) to increase the success rate of mycorrhizal formation, with an emphasis on rooting and lateral root formation (find ways to stimulate beech having more lateral roots in a short time and leave it growing until the influence removed naturally). [SOURCE: Page_126]

The differences between strains 1.093 and 1.058 for C. geophilum were notable. While both strains shared similar rDNA ITS sequences, only approximately 60% of sequencing reads from 1.093 could be mapped to the 1.058 reference genome, suggesting significant genetic divergence. Phylogenetically, C. geophilum 1.093 belonged to clade 6, which was geographically restricted, whereas 1.058 was more widely distributed. The traits and behaviors of these strains also differ: 1.093 forms endophytic structures in beech roots but does not degrade cellophane or herbaceous plant root hairs (my observation during the experiments), whereas 1.058 degrades cellophane and forms mycorrhizas with herbaceous plants. [SOURCE: Page_126]

These differences suggest that these two strains represent distinct species in the cryptic Cenococcum species complex. Comparative genomics has shown that genes involved in plant cell wall degradation, particularly lignin, cellulose, and hemicellulose degradation, were reduced during the evolutionary shift from saprotrophic to symbiotic lifestyles in ectomycorrhizal fungi (Kohler et al., 2015; Miyauchi et al., 2020). Strain 1.093 may have lost more saprotrophic genes, becoming more symbiotic, whereas 1.058 may retain more saprotrophic characteristics. Whole-genome sequencing of 1.093 is planned to further explore these differences and improve our understanding of C. geophilum physiology and symbiotic ability. Future studies should screen additional C. geophilum strains for their drought tolerance and explore possible correlations between phylogenetic placement, genomic features, and drought resilience. [SOURCE: Page_126]

117 [SOURCE: Page_126]

Résumé Ces dernières années, les événements climatiques extrêmes, en particulier les sécheresses printanières et estivales prolongées, représentent des risques importants pour les forêts, conduisant à des dépérissements qui touchent de nombreuses espèces. En particulier, ces changements climatiques entraî nent une diminution des populations de hêtre (Fagus sylvatica L.) à travers l'Europe. Par conséquent, le développement de stratégies visant à renforcer la résilience des hêtres face à la sécheresse est devenu crucial. Notre recherche a explorési Cenococcum geophilum, un champignon ascomycète ectomycorhizien cosmopolite, pouvait tolérer des conditions de sécheresse et aider ses plantes hôtes, en particulier le hêtre, às'adapter au manque d'eau. Par criblage phénotypique de souches fongiques, nous avons identifié une souche de C. geophilum, potentiellement tolérante àla sécheresse, en testant la croissance des isolats sur un milieu de croissance contenant des concentrations accrues de polyéthylène glycol (PEG) comme substitut du stress hydrique du sol ; le PEG augmentant la pression osmotique des cellules. Nous avons ensuite analyséle transcriptome de cette souche cultivésur PEG àdifférents intervalles et évaluéla récupération de son expression génique après un retour aux conditions normales. Six heures après le traitement au PEG, l'expression génique a changé de manière spectaculaire, activant des voies connues d'adaptation au stress osmotique et de détoxification. Le transcriptome de l'isolat est revenu àson état initial deux jours après son transfert sur un milieu de culture dépourvu de PEG. Étant donné la tolérance de l'isolat à des changements majeurs de pression osmotique et sa récupération rapide, nous avons mené une expérience en serre pour déterminer si une inoculation mycorhizienne contrôlée avec cette souche pouvait améliorer la résistance des plantules de hêtre àla suspension de l'irrigation. Bien que la mycorhization contrôlée ait augmentéle taux de survie des semis, les isolats naturels de C. geophilum présents dans le sol des cultures ont surpassénotre souche sélectionnée, probablement en raison de la brève durée de l'expérience et de la formation limitée de nouvelles racines latérales. Pour approfondir les mécanismes moléculaires de la tolérance à la sécheresse de l’association symbiotique Cenococcum-Fagus, nous avons tenté de développer un système in vitro de culture reposant sur la micro-propagation de plantules de hêtre. Cependant, toutes les lignées de hêtre testées ont montré une formation réduite de racines latérales et de faibles taux de colonisation avec cinq champignons ectomycorhiziens, y compris C. geophilum. Bien qu’aucune ectomycorhize typique n’a pu être observé, nous avons mise en évidence par microscopie confocale la formation de structures intraradiculaires semblables àcelles développées par les champignons endophytes. Pour de futures études, nous recommandons de sélectionner des lignées de hêtre avec de meilleures capacités d'enracinement (Chapitre 4) et d'élargir le nombre d'isolats de C. geophilum testés (Chapitres 3 et 4), en isolant des souches adaptées localement. [SOURCE: Page_127]

118 [SOURCE: Page_127]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_128]

Recently, extreme climatic events, such as extended drought periods, cause serious threats to forest ecosystems. These climate changes is leading to a decline in beech (Fagus sylvatica L.) populations across Europe. Developing forest management approaches to enhance the drought resistance of beech trees has become increasingly important. [SOURCE: Page_128]

In this study, we investigated whether the genus Cenococcum, host generalist, worldwide spreading ectomycorrhizal fungus and present in extreme environments, can survive and support its host (especially European beech) to adapt to drought conditions. To study it, we screened Cenococcum isolates on medium with increasing water potential and identified a drought tolerant isolate. Then the transcriptome of this isolate under drought condition (mimicked by polyethylene glycol (PEG) treatment) at different timepoints was investigated as well as its ability to recover after return to control condition. As early as six hours after PEG treatment, gene expression dramatically changed by inducing well-known adaptation pathways to osmotic stress and detoxification. Within two days after transferring the isolate back to the no drought treatment medium its transcriptome is similar to the non-treated. Since the isolate is drought tolerant and recovered fast after transfer to no drought stress condition, we tested whether controlled mycorrhization by this isolate in the greenhouse could improve the resistance of young beech trees to the suspension of irrigation. In our experiment controlled mycorrhization increased the survival rate of the young beech trees, but probably Cenococcum isolates present in the natural soil have a better performance (higher colonization rates) then the selected strain, partly probably due to the short experimental time and the lack of new lateral roots formed. To be able to study the molecular mechanisms of drought tolerance in Cenococcum- European beech symbiosis, we started to develop an in vitro system. Unfortunately, all tested European beech lines have a low ability to form lateral roots and are poorly colonized by five ectomycorrhizal fungi tested. Although no typical ectomycorrhiza was formed, we detected an endophyte-like structure formed by Cenococcum in the roots of in vitro European beech. [SOURCE: Page_128]

For future research, we suggest to screen for better rooting beech lines (Chapter 4) and to enlarge the number of Cenococcum isolates (Chapter 3 and 4) e.g. trapping locally adapted strains. [SOURCE: Page_128]

119 [SOURCE: Page_128]
